Variance Reduction based Experience Replay for Policy Optimization
Hua Zheng, Wei Xie, M. Ben Feng

Abstract
For reinforcement learning on complex stochastic systems, it is desirable to effectively leverage the information from historical samples collected in previous iterations to accelerate policy optimization. Classical experience replay, while effective, treats all observations uniformly, neglecting their relative importance. To address this limitation, we introduce a novel Variance Reduction Experience Replay (VRER) framework, enabling the selective reuse of relevant samples to improve policy gradient estimation. VRER, as an adaptable method that can seamlessly integrate with different policy optimization algorithms, forms the foundation of our sample efficient off-policy learning algorithm known as Policy Gradient with VRER (PG-VRER). Furthermore, the lack of a rigorous understanding of the experience replay approach in the literature motivates us to introduce a novel theoretical framework that accounts for sample dependencies induced by Markovian noise and behavior policy interdependencies. This framework is then employed to analyze the finite-time convergence of the proposed PG-VRER algorithm, revealing a crucial bias-variance trade-off in policy gradient estimation: the reuse of older experience tends to introduce a larger bias while simultaneously reducing gradient estimation variance. Extensive experiments have shown that VRER offers a notable and consistent acceleration in learning optimal policies and enhances the performance of state-of-the-art (SOTA) policy optimization approaches.



Keywords
Reinforcement Learning
\cdot

Policy Optimization
\cdot

Important Sampling
\cdot

Variance Reduction
\cdot

Experience Replay
\cdot

Markov Decision Process

1
Introduction

In recent years, various policy optimization approaches are developed to solve challenging control problems in healthcare
(
Yu et al. 2021
;
Zheng et al. 2021b
)
, continuous control tasks
(
Lillicrap et al. 2016
;
Schulman et al. 2015
;
Schulman et al. 2017
)
, and biomanufacturing
(
Zheng et al. 2023
)
.
These approaches often consider parametric policies and search for optimal solution through policy gradient approach
(
Sutton and Barto 2018
)
, whose performance and convergence crucially depends on the accuracy of gradient estimation.
Reusing historical samples is one way to improve gradient estimation, especially when historical data is scarce.
In this paper, we address an important question in policy optimization methods:
How to intelligently select and reuse historical samples to accelerate the learning of the optimal policy for complex stochastic systems?

According to the different basic unit of historical samples to reuse, policy gradient (PG)
algorithms can be classified into episode-based and step-based approaches
(
Metelli et al. 2020
)
.
Episode-based approaches reuse historical trajectories through importance sampling (IS) strategy accounting for the distributional difference induced by the target and behavior policies.
In this case, the importance sampling weight is built on the product of likelihood ratios (LR) of state-action transitions occurring within each process trajectory. As a result, the likelihood-ratio-weighted observations can have large or even infinite variance, especially for problems with long planning horizons
(
Schlegel et al. 2019
)
.
On the other hand, the step-based approaches take individual state-action transitions as the basic reuse units.
This overcomes the limitation of episode-based approaches, provides a more flexible reuse strategy, and supports process online control.

In this paper, we will develop a variance-reduction based experience replay framework applicable to step-based approaches.

A noticeable limitation of current reinforcement learning (RL) techniques is the low sample efficiency
(
Botvinick et al. 2019
)
. This challenge becomes especially pronounced in complex stochastic systems, such as healthcare
(
Hall et al. 2012
;
Zheng et al. 2021a
)
and biopharmaceutical manufacturing
(
Zheng et al. 2023
)
, where each real or simulated experiment can be financially or computationally expensive. Another growing application of RL is RLHF, short for Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback
(
Christiano et al. 2017
)
, used extensively in large language model-based chat models
(
Workshop et al. 2022
;
Touvron et al. 2023
)
to align with human preferences. However, it’s crucial to underscore that RLHF heavily depends on accumulating significant volumes of expensive human feedback data. In light of these considerations, it becomes important to maximize the utilization of all available information when addressing RL optimization problems. On-policy methods only utilize newly generated samples to estimate the policy gradient for each policy update.
Ignoring the relevant information carried with historical samples can lead to low sample efficiency and high uncertainty in policy gradient estimation.

Fortunately, this information loss can be reduced through a combination of experience replay (ER)
(
Lin 1992
;
Mnih et al. 2015
;
Wang et al. 2017
)
and off-policy optimization methods, which can store and “replay" past relevant experiences to accelerate the search for optimal policy. However, off-policy methods introduce additional sources of approximation, as estimating the gradient requires using samples collected with old behavior policies. A common approach is to employ importance sampling
(
Owen 2013
;
Hesterberg 1988
)
to address this likelihood mismatch induced by the target policy that we want to assess and the behavioral
policy under which past samples were collected. Nevertheless, directly applying the likelihood ratio (LR) can lead to
increased variance

(
Owen 2013
;
Zheng et al. 2020
)
, especially when behavior policies significantly differ from the target policies.

Despite its empirical success, the precise mechanisms governing the impact of factors like buffer size, policy age, and the mixing rate of the environment on the performance of RL algorithms utilizing ER are not well understood. To address this gap, we present a novel theoretical framework, aimed at providing insights into the inner workings of experience replay. This framework unveils a key variance-bias trade-off: replaying older samples introduces a higher bias into the learning process while replaying a larger number of historical samples results in reduced gradient variance. Understanding this trade-off is crucial, as it directly influences the convergence rate of ER-based policy optimization algorithms. Furthermore, our study establishes a fundamental connection between the convergence rate and the mixing rate of the environment, shedding light on the interplay between these essential components. This theoretical foundation not only advances our comprehension of experience replay but also offers valuable guidance for optimizing RL algorithms.

Driven by our novel theoretical framework and the practical needs of sample-efficient policy optimization algorithms, we propose the variance reduction based experience replay (VRER): a versatile framework designed to reduce the variance of policy gradient estimation and speed up the search for optimal policy by selectively reuse historical samples.

As policy optimization proceeds, this approach automatically identifies and replays the most relevant historical samples, effectively reducing policy gradient variance.
Furthermore, an efficient approximate selection rule is introduced to alleviate the computational demands of the proposed selection procedure.
Here, we summarize the key contributions and benefits of the proposed framework.

•

We propose a VRER framework for policy gradient optimization. It can accelerate the learning of optimal policy for step-based policy optimization algorithms. The proposed VRER can intelligently select and reuse the most relevant historical samples to improve sample efficiency and estimation accuracy of policy gradient.

•

We develop a novel finite-time convergence analysis for experience replay, accounting for sample dependence induced by Markovian noise and behavior policy interdependence. This theoretical framework reveals the effectiveness of experience replay accounting for the impact of behavior policy age, state transitions, and buffer size on RL performance through a bias-variance trade-off in policy gradients. Applying this theory to the proposed PG-VRER allows us to examine its finite-time convergence.

•

Extensive empirical studies demonstrate that the proposed VRER framework can efficiently utilize past samples, reduce the policy gradient estimation variance, and accelerate the learning of optimal policy for complex stochastic systems.

The organization of this paper is as follows.
We review the most related literature studies in Section
2
, and
present the problem description and assumptions of policy gradient optimization for infinite horizon Markov decision processes (MDPs)
in Section
3
. We propose the IS-based policy gradient estimators for step-based algorithms in Section
4
. Then, we establish a new theoretical framework for the experience replay in Section
5
. This framework analyzes the bias-variance trade-off in policy gradient estimation due to the dependency of state-action pairs and distributional shifts between stationary distributions of evolving policies. Then, we develop the computationally efficient selection rules and propose generic VRER based policy gradient optimization in Section
6
. In Section
7
,
we provide a finite-time convergence analysis of PG-VRER and show the asymptotic properties of experience replay.
We conclude this paper with a comprehensive empirical study on the proposed framework in Section
8
. The implementation of VRER can be found at
GitHub
.

2
Related Works

The goal of RL is to learn the optimal policy through dynamic interactions with the systems of interest to achieve the best reward

(
Sutton and Barto 2018
)
. Stochastic gradient
approaches are often used to solve RL problems
(
Sutton et al. 1999a
)
.
The study of policy optimization can be traced back to REINFORCE, also known as vanilla policy gradient (VPG)
(
Williams 1992
)
. Later advancements, such as the introduction of value function approximations to policy optimization, gave rise to actor-critic methods
(
Konda and Tsitsiklis 1999
;
Sutton et al. 1999b
)
. In recent years, numerous approaches have emerged to enhance sample efficiency, scalability, and convergence speed, including Asynchronous Advantage Actor-Critic (A3C)
(
Mnih et al. 2016
)
, Soft Actor-Critic (SAC)
(
Haarnoja et al. 2018
)
, trust region policy optimization (TRPO)
(
Schulman et al. 2015
)
, and the proximal policy optimization (PPO)
(
Schulman et al. 2017
)
. An important perspective of policy gradient methods is to prevent dramatic updates in policy parametric space. Driven by this principle, TRPO considers a surrogate objective function subject to the trust region constraint which enforces the distance between old policy and new update measured by KL-divergence to be small enough. Following the similar idea, PPO truncates the likelihood ratio of new and old candidates
to discourage excessively large policy updates.

Importance sampling (IS) has been widely applied to improve policy optimization. Traditionally, it was often used for off-policy value evaluation and policy correction through likelihood ratio between the target policy and the behavior policy
(
Jiang and Li 2016
;
Precup 2000
;
Degris et al. 2012
)
. However, IS estimators can suffer from large
variance, which motivates the development of variance reduction techniques
(
Mahmood and Sutton 2015
;
Wu et al. 2018
;
Lyu et al. 2020
)
. For example,
Espeholt et al. 2018
presents an IS weighted actor-critic architecture that adopts
“retrace"
(
Munos et al. 2016
)
, a method truncating IS weight with an upper bound to resolve high variance issue.
Metelli et al. 2020
propose the Policy Optimization via Importance Sampling (POIS) approach which
optimizes a surrogate objective function accounting for the trade-off between the estimated
performance improvement and variance inflation induced by IS.
Also, IS is studied in the simulation literature; for example
Feng and Staum 2017
;
Dong et al. 2018
propose a green simulation approach that reuses outputs from past simulation experiments to create a metamodel predicting system mean response and guiding the design of experiments.

However, two significant challenges arise in the application of IS to step-based policy optimization algorithms: Markovian noise and the interdependencies among behavior policies. Markovian noise represents a common obstacle for step-based algorithms since it induces sample correlation that complicates the optimization process.
In addition, the issue of interdependencies between behavior policies, as highlighted by the studies, e.g.,
Eckman and Henderson 2018
and
Liu and Zhou 2020
in the context of simulation optimizations, suggests that the IS-based gradient estimator could incur bias due to dependencies across different optimization iterations.
Following this line of work,
Lin and Zhou 2023
used the Ordinary Differential Equation (ODE) method to study the asymptotic convergence of the policy gradient algorithm by reusing history observations. However, their analysis only considers the impact of policy interdependencies but overlooks the impact of Markovian noise by assuming that the observations
collected in each iteration are independently sampled from a discounted occupancy measure.

Experience replay (ER) and its extension, such as prioritized experience replay
(
Schaul et al. 2016
)
, are often used in the policy gradient and RL algorithms to reduce data correlation and improve sample efficiency.
Zhang and Sutton 2017
examined the relationship between replay buffer size and agent performance. They found that too small or too large replay buffers negatively affected performance in three specific tasks.
Fu et al. 2019
highlighted the sensitivity of agent performance to the ratio of environment steps to gradient steps.
van Hasselt et al. 2019
experimented with this ratio and batch sizes for a more efficient Rainbow version.
Fedus et al. 2020
studied the effect of replay capacity, buffer size, and policy age. Furthermore, the refinement and deeper understanding of experience replay algorithms remains active with contributions from various other studies
(
Sun et al. 2020
;
Lee et al. 2019
;
Novati and Koumoutsakos 2019
;
Pan et al. 2022
)
.

The proposed VRER method stands out from existing approaches by tackling the problems of biased policy gradient estimation and inflated variance, supported by our novel and rigorous theoretical analysis on bias-variance trade-off. This approach incorporates a selection rule and a managed buffer capacity that collectively reduce the variance induced by reusing correlated historical samples and mitigate bias due to the use of outdated samples. Furthermore, this selection process imposes a control on the magnitude of parameter updates, ensuring that the discrepancy between successive policies remains constrained.

3
Problem Description

In this section, we first formulate infinite-horizon Markov decision processes (MDPs) in Section
3.1
and review policy gradient theorem and gradient update rule in Section
3.2
.
Then we summarize the regularity assumptions and conditions and present some established lemmas for policy gradient optimizations in Section
3.3
.

Notation
: We use
\rho(x,y)=\mbox{Cov}\left(x,y\right)/\left(\sqrt{{\rm Var}(x)}\sqrt{{\rm Var}(y)}\right)
denote the correlation coefficient between random variable
x
and
y
. Let
{\rm Diag}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
denote the
n\times n
matrix with diagonal entries
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
, and zero off-diagonal elements.
For any
(d\times d)
matrix
A
, the trace used in the paper is defined as
{\rm Tr}({A})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}A_{i,i}
. We consider
L_{2}
norm for any
d
-dimensional vector
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, i.e.,
\|\boldsymbol{x}\|\vcentcolon=\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{2}=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{2}}
. Let
P
and
Q
be two probability measures, and
p
and
q
denote their density functions. The total variation (TV) distance between two probability measure
P
and
Q
on the sample space
\mathcal{X}

is defined
as
\|P-Q\|_{TV}=1/2\int_{\mathcal{X}}|P(dx)-Q(dx)|=1/2\int_{\mathcal{X}}|p(x)-q(x)|dx
. In what follows,

\lceil x\rceil
denotes the smallest integer greater than or equal to
x
,
\lfloor x\rfloor
denotes the largest integer less than or equal to
x
, and
[N]
denotes the set of integers
\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
.

3.1
Markov Decision Process

We formulate the problem of interest as an infinite-horizon discounted MDP specified by
(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},r,\mathbb{P},\gamma,\boldsymbol{s}_{1})
, where
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{A}

denote the state and action space.
At any time
t
, the agent observes the state
\boldsymbol{s}_{t}\in\mathcal{S}
, takes an action
\boldsymbol{a}_{t}\in\mathcal{A}
,
and receives a reward
r(\boldsymbol{s}_{t},\boldsymbol{a}_{t})\in\mathbb{R}
. In this study, we consider stochastic policy
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}:\mathcal{S}\mapsto\mathcal{A}
, defined as a mapping from state space to the action and it is parameterized by
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{a}_{t}\sim\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s}_{t})
.
The state transition is specified by a probability model
\mathbb{P}
, i.e.,
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t+1}\in\cdot|\boldsymbol{s}_{t},\boldsymbol{a}_{t})
with probability density function
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t}=\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}|\boldsymbol{s}_{t},\boldsymbol{a}_{t})=p(\boldsymbol{s}_{t}=\boldsymbol{s}|\boldsymbol{s}_{t},\boldsymbol{a}_{t})
.
We use
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t}\in\cdot|\boldsymbol{s}_{1},\boldsymbol{\theta})
to represent the
t
-step state transition measure under a fixed behavior policy
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
. By an abuse of notation,
we use
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t}\in\cdot|\boldsymbol{s}_{1})
to represent the
t
-step state transition measure under different behavior policies
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}},\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}},\ldots,\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t-1}}
.

The performance of any candidate policy
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is evaluated in terms of the expected return, i.e., the
expected discounted sum of the rewards

R(\boldsymbol{\tau})\vcentcolon=\sum^{H+1}_{t=1}\gamma^{t}r(\boldsymbol{s}_{t},\boldsymbol{a}_{t})
collected along the trajectory
\boldsymbol{\tau}=(\boldsymbol{s}_{1},\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\boldsymbol{s}_{2},\ldots,\boldsymbol{s}_{H+1})
with length
H
, where
\gamma\in(0,1)
denotes the discount factor. Let
R_{t}^{\gamma}(H)\vcentcolon=\sum^{H+1}_{t^{\prime}=t}\gamma^{t^{\prime}-t}r(\boldsymbol{s}_{t^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{a}_{t^{\prime}})
denote the total discounted reward from time-step
t

onwards. Our goal is to find the optimal policy, denoted by
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}
, maximizing the expected reward in long term, i.e.,

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta}~J(\boldsymbol{\theta})={\mathbb{E}}\left[\left.\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t-1}r(\boldsymbol{s}_{t},\boldsymbol{a}_{t})\right|\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\right]={\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{s}\sim d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\boldsymbol{s}),\boldsymbol{a}\sim\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})}[r(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})],

(1)

where
\Theta
represents the policy parameter space and
d^{{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}}(\boldsymbol{s})
is the state stationary distribution induced by the policy
{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}
, defined as

d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\boldsymbol{s})=(1-\gamma)\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t-1}p(\boldsymbol{s}_{t}=\boldsymbol{s}|\boldsymbol{s}_{1};{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}).

We denote
the state-occupancy
measure of state-action pair by

d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})=\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\boldsymbol{s})
.

Similarly, given policy
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, we define the state-value function starting with state
\boldsymbol{s}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle V^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle=\lim_{H\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left.R^{\gamma}_{1}(H)\right|\boldsymbol{s}_{1}=\boldsymbol{s};{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\right]={\mathbb{E}}\left[\left.\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t-1}r(\boldsymbol{s}_{t},\boldsymbol{a}_{t})\right|\boldsymbol{s}_{1}=\boldsymbol{s};{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\right],

and the action-value function starting with state-action pair
(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})

\displaystyle=\lim_{H\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left.R^{\gamma}_{1}(H)\right|\boldsymbol{s}_{1}=\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a}_{1}=\boldsymbol{a};{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\right]={\mathbb{E}}\left[\left.\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t-1}r(\boldsymbol{s}_{t},\boldsymbol{a}_{t})\right|\boldsymbol{s}_{1}=\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a}_{1}=\boldsymbol{a};\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\right].

3.2
Generic Policy Gradient

Stochastic policy gradient ascent is a popular method to solve the RL optimization problem (
1
). At each
k
-th iteration, we iteratively update the policy parameters by

\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}+\eta_{k}\widehat{\nabla}J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}),

(2)

where
\eta_{k}
is learning rate or step size and
\widehat{\nabla}J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})
is an estimator of policy gradient
\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})
.
For convenience,
\nabla
denotes the gradient with respect to
\boldsymbol{\theta}
unless specified otherwise.

Under some regular conditions
used to change the order of gradient and expectation

(
Sutton et al. 1999b
;
Williams 1992
)
,
the generic policy gradient can be written as,

\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta})={\mathbb{E}}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\sim d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\cdot,\cdot)}[g(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})|\boldsymbol{\theta}]={\mathbb{E}}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\sim d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\cdot,\cdot)}\left[A^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\nabla\log\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})\right]

(3)

where
A^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\vcentcolon=Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})-V^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\boldsymbol{s})
is called
advantage
and it intuitively measures the extra reward that the agent can obtain by taking a particular action
\boldsymbol{a}
at state
\boldsymbol{s}
. Also, the scenario-based policy gradient estimate
in (
3
) is

g\left(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)\vcentcolon=A^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\nabla\log\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s}).

(4)

Modern policy optimization algorithms have shifted from frequent policy updates to performing gradient updates after multiple interaction steps
with the environment. Thus, the classical
policy gradient (PG) estimator
in the
k
-th iteration is given by the sample average of
n
new generated samples, i.e.,

\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\vcentcolon=\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}g\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{(k,j)},\boldsymbol{a}^{(k,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right),

(5)

where
\left({\boldsymbol{s}}^{(k,j)},{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(k,j)}\right)
represents the
j
-th sample collected at the
k
-th iteration. Throughout this paper, we consider a general algorithmic setting: at each
k
-th iteration, we collect
n
new samples (i.e., fixed-length trajectory segments collected from one or multiple environments)
by following the latest policy
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}
to estimate and optimize the objective (
1
).

3.3
Regularity Conditions for Generic Policy Gradient Estimation

We summarize the standard assumptions and conditions for
the regularity of generic policy gradient and MDPs, which will be used throughout this paper.

A.1

Suppose the reward and policy functions satisfy the following regularity conditions.

(i)

The absolute value of the reward
r(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})
is bounded uniformly, i.e., there exists a constant, say
U_{r}>0
such that
|r(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})|\leq U_{r}
for any
(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
.

(ii)

The score function is Lipschitz continuous and has bounded norm; and the policy
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is differentiable and Lipschitz continuous with respect to
\boldsymbol{\theta}
and has bounded likelihood ratio,
i.e., for any
(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, there exist positive constant bounds, denoted by
L_{\Theta},U_{\Theta}
,
U_{\pi}
, and
U_{L}<\infty
, such that

\displaystyle\|\nabla\log\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})-\nabla\log\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})\|\leq L_{\Theta}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\|\text{ for any $\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}$ and $\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}$};

(6)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\log\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})\|\leq U_{\Theta}\text{ for any $\boldsymbol{\theta}$};

(7)

\displaystyle\|\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\cdot|\boldsymbol{s})-\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}(\cdot|\boldsymbol{s})\|_{TV}\leq{U_{\pi}}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\|;

(8)

\displaystyle\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})/\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})<U_{L}\ \text{ almost everywhere for any $\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}$ and $\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}$}.

(9)

A.2

(Uniform Ergodicity)
For a fixed
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, denote
d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\cdot)
as the stationary distribution of
an infinite-horizon MDP generated by the rule, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{a}_{t}\sim\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\cdot|\boldsymbol{s}_{t})
and
\boldsymbol{s}_{t+1}\sim p(\cdot|\boldsymbol{s}_{t},\boldsymbol{a}_{t})
.
There exists a decreasing function
\varphi(t)>0
such that:

\left\|\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{s}_{t}\in\cdot|\boldsymbol{s}_{1}=\boldsymbol{s})-d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\cdot)\right\|_{TV}\leq\varphi(t),\forall t\geq 1,\forall\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathcal{S}.

where
\varphi(t)=\kappa_{0}\kappa^{t}
for some constants
\kappa_{0}>0
and
\kappa\in(0,1)
.

The uniform bound assumption for reward function in
A.1
(i) is commonly used in most RL studies
(
Wu et al. 2020
;
Zhang et al. 2020
;
Kumar et al. 2019
)
.
The first two items in
A.1
(ii) are standard assumptions on
the regularity of the MDP problem and the parameterized policy, which are the same conditions used in many recent studies
(
Wu et al. 2020
;
Zhang et al. 2020
;
Kumar et al. 2019
)
. The third item in
A.1
(ii) is adopted from
Xu et al. 2020

which holds for any smooth policy with bounded action space and Gaussian policy; see justification in
Xu et al. 2020
.
The fourth item of Assumption
A.1
(ii) on likelihood ratios is incorporated to prevent the mean and variance of gradient estimates from becoming unbounded. In practice, this assumption can be easily satisfied by clipping the likelihood ratio between two policies
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})/\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})
. The uniform ergodicity assumption
A.2
is a commonly used assumption that implies long-term stability in the sense that the Markov chain has a stationary distribution to which it will converge, regardless of the initial state. This assumption has been adopted by
Xu et al. 2020
;
Wu et al. 2020
;
Bhandari et al. 2018
;
Zou et al. 2019
, which holds for any time-homogeneous Markov chain with finite-state space or any uniformly ergodic Markov chain with general state space.

The uniform boundedness of the reward function
r(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})
in Assumption
A.1
(i) also implies that the absolute value of the Q-function is upper bounded
by
\frac{U_{r}}{1-\gamma}
, since by definition

|Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})|\leq\sum^{\infty}_{t=1}\gamma^{t-1}U_{r}=\frac{U_{r}}{1-\gamma},\text{ for any $(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}$}.

(10)

The same bound also applies for
V^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\boldsymbol{s})
for any
\boldsymbol{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathcal{S}
, i.e.,

|V^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\boldsymbol{s})|=|{\mathbb{E}}\left[Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\right]|\leq{\mathbb{E}}\left[|Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})|\right]\leq\sum^{\infty}_{t=1}\gamma^{t-1}U_{r}=\frac{U_{r}}{1-\gamma}.

(11)

This applies to the objective
J(\boldsymbol{\theta})
because
|J(\boldsymbol{\theta})|=|{\mathbb{E}}[V^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\boldsymbol{s})]|\leq\frac{U_{r}}{1-\gamma}
.
Thus, we have the bound of the objective as
U_{J}\vcentcolon=\frac{U_{r}}{1-\gamma}
.

Under Assumption
A.1
, we can establish the Lipschitz continuity of the policy gradient
\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta})
as shown in
Lemma
1
. The proof is deferred to Lemma 3.2 in
Zhang et al. 2020
.
Lemma
1
is another essential condition to ensure the convergence of many gradient-based algorithms; see for example
Niu et al. 2011
;
Reddi et al. 2016
;
Nemirovski et al. 2009
;
Li and Orabona 2019
. It implies that the policy gradient cannot change abruptly, i.e.,
|J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})-J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})-\langle\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}),\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}\rangle|\leq L\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}\rVert^{2}
(
Nesterov 2003
, Lemma 1.2.3).

Lemma 1

(
Zhang et al. 2020
, Lemma 3.2)
.

Under Assumption
A.1
, the policy gradient
\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is Lipschitz continuous, i.e., for any
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\in\Theta
, there exists a constant
L>0
s.t.

\|\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\|\leq L\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\rVert.

At the end of this section, we present Lemma
2
that establishes the boundedness of policy gradient and its stochastic estimate.
There exists a constant
M>0
such that the
L_{2}
norm of the scenario-based policy gradient estimate is bounded, i.e.,
\|g(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{\theta})\|\leq M
.

Lemma 2

(Boundedness of Stochastic Policy Gradients)
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, the norm of the policy gradient
\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta})
and its scenario-based stochastic estimate
g(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{\theta})
is bounded, i.e.,

\|\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta})\|\leq M\text{ and }\|g\left(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\|\leq M,

where
M=\frac{2U_{r}U_{\Theta}}{1-\gamma}
.

Proof.

From Eq. (
4
), we have the scenario-based stochastic gradient estimate

\|g\left(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\|\leq|Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})-V^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\boldsymbol{s})|\|\nabla\log\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})\|\leq\frac{2U_{r}U_{\Theta}}{1-\gamma}.

(12)

Step (
12
) follows by applying Assumption
A.1
(ii) and the boundedness of value functions in Eq. (
10
) and (
11
). Let
M=\frac{2U_{r}U_{\Theta}}{1-\gamma}
. The boundedness of policy gradient
\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta})
follows the fact that

\|\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta})\|=\|{\mathbb{E}}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\sim d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\cdot,\cdot)}[g(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{\theta})]\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\star)}}{{\leq}}{\mathbb{E}}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\sim d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\cdot,\cdot)}[\|g(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{\theta})\|]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\star\star)}}{{\leq}}{\mathbb{E}}[M]=M,

where step (
\star
) follows by applying Jensen’s inequality and
(\star\star)
follows by applying (
12
).
∎

In recent years, a variety of policy gradient algorithms have been proposed, each with a different form of policy gradient.
Interested readers for other variants are referred to
Sutton and Barto 2018
and
Schulman et al. 2016
for
the summary of general policy gradient algorithms.
We highlight the versatility of our proposed VRER approach, emphasizing its compatibility with a broad range of policy gradient optimization algorithms.
In the empirical study in Section
8
, we demonstrate this generality by using three SOTA algorithms.

4
Likelihood Ratio Based Policy Gradient Estimation

At any
k
-th iteration, in this section, we describe how to utilize IS or likelihood ratio (LR) to improve the estimation of
policy gradient
\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})
through reusing the selected historical samples.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{k}
denote the set of all behavioral distributions that have been visited by the beginning of the
k
-th iteration.
Let
\mathcal{U}_{k}
be
a reuse set
with
\mathcal{U}_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{k}
including the MDP model candidates
whose historical samples are selected and reused for improving the estimation of policy gradient
\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})
. Denote its cardinality as
|\mathcal{U}_{k}|
.
For discussions in this section, we assume the reuse set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
is given. We will present how to select it in Section
6
.

4.1
Step-based Policy Optimization via Importance Sampling

The IS technique has been successfully applied to the episode-based policy optimization for the finite-horizon MDP
(
Zheng et al. 2020
;
Metelli et al. 2020
)
.
However, extending its application to step-based methods in infinite-horizon MDPs introduces significant theoretical challenges. Specifically, in contrast to episode-based approaches, the IS identity does not hold in step-based policy optimization for infinite-horizon MDPs, because state-action pairs are sequentially sampled from the environment, rather than being independently drawn from a stationary distribution.

Due to Markovian noise adhering to the Markov property of state transition dynamics
p(\boldsymbol{s}_{t+1}|\boldsymbol{s}_{t},\boldsymbol{a}_{t})
, this sequential sampling introduces
sample dependencies that result in biased LR-based estimators of the policy gradient.

To clarify this, consider the task of estimating the gradient under the target policy
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}
after running
k
timesteps or iterations starting from the initial state
\boldsymbol{s}_{1}
by using the historical state-action sample
(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})
collected at the
i
-th iteration under a behavior policy
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}
. Then taking the expectation shows the biasedness of this LR gradient estimator, i.e.,

{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{s}\sim\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{s}_{i}\in\cdot|\boldsymbol{s}_{1}),\boldsymbol{a}\sim\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}(\cdot|\boldsymbol{s})}\left[\varrho_{i,k}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})g(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]={\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{s}\sim\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{s}_{k}\in\cdot|\boldsymbol{s}_{1}),\boldsymbol{a}\sim\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}(\cdot|\boldsymbol{s})}\left[g(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]\neq\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})

(13)

where
\varrho_{i,k}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})=\frac{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{s}_{k}=\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}|\boldsymbol{s}_{1})\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})}{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{s}_{i}=\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}|\boldsymbol{s}_{1})\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})}
and
\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})={\mathbb{E}}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\sim d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}}(\cdot,\cdot)}[g(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}]
. Notice that we write the probability density function of
k
-step transition under a sequence of evolving behavior policies
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k-1})
induced by the optimization search as
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{s}_{k}=\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}|\boldsymbol{s}_{1})\vcentcolon=\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{s}_{k}=\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}|\boldsymbol{s}_{1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k-1})
. In step-based off-policy optimization methods
(
Degris et al. 2012
;
Meng et al. 2023
)
, the likelihood ratio
\varrho_{i,k}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})
is often simplified to
f_{i,k}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\vcentcolon=\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})}
. This simplification further invalidates the first equality of (
13
), as the likelihood ratio is reduced to merely the ratio of the target to the behavior policy. Although biased, this simplified estimator has been widely used due to its simplicity
(
Degris et al. 2012
)
.

4.2
Likelihood Ratio (LR) Policy Gradient Estimator

The policy gradient
\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})
can be estimated by the individual likelihood ratio (LR) estimator, i.e., using
n
historical samples collected under the behavior policy
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}
with
i<k
,

\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR}_{i,k}=\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{j=1}f_{i,k}\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}\right)g\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)~\mbox{with}~f_{i,k}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})=\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})}.

(14)

The likelihood ratio
f_{i,k}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})
weights the historical samples
to account for
the mismatch between
the behavior and target policies specified by parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}
.

One way to reuse all the observations associated with the behavioral MDP distributions included in the reuse set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
is to average all individual LR estimators for
{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}
, which we call the
likelihood ratio (LR) policy gradient estimator
,

\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR}_{k}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR}_{i,k}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|n}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{n}_{j=1}\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)})}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)})}g\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right).

(15)

For simplification, we allocate a constant number of replications (i.e.,
n
) for each visit at
\boldsymbol{\theta}
.

4.3
Clipped Likelihood Ratio (CLR) Policy Gradient Estimator

Though the individual/average LR estimators (
14
) and (
15
) are popular, their variances could be large or even infinite, as the likelihood ratio
f_{i,k}(\cdot,\cdot)
can be large or unbounded
(
Veach and Guibas 1995
)
. A technique for mitigating this problem is weight clipping
(
Ionides 2008
)
, which truncates the LR by applying the operator

\min(f_{i,k}(\cdot,\cdot),U_{f})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}J^{CLR}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\widehat{\nabla}J^{CLR}_{i,k},

(16)

\displaystyle\mbox{ with }\widehat{\nabla}J^{CLR}_{i,k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{j=1}\min\left(\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)})}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)})},U_{f}\right)g\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right).

(17)

Here
U_{f}
is a constant greater than 1, which effectively caps the likelihood ratio
at a specified limit, thus controlling the variance. This approach provides a simple and practical solution to manage the potentially extreme values that the likelihood ratio might assume.
Similarly, we refer to
\widehat{\nabla}J^{CLR}_{i,k}
and
\widehat{\nabla}J^{CLR}_{k}
in (
17
) and (
16
) as the individual and average clipped likelihood ratio (CLR) policy gradient estimators.

We conclude this section by
pointing out that we proceed with both theoretical and experimental analysis using biased estimators in (
15
) and (
16
)
in the following sections.

5
Variance-Bias Analysis in Experience Replay (ER)

While numerous studies have empirically examined the influence of hyperparameters of the ER, such as policy age, buffer size, and replay capacity, there is a notable absence of a comprehensive theoretical study on the variance-bias trade-off in sample reuse and LR-based policy gradient estimation. This section addresses this gap by introducing a new theoretical framework. It examines how sample dependence, resulting from Markovian noise, ER, and policy updates, affects the Bias (Section
5.1
) and Variance (Section
5.2
) of LR/CLR policy gradient estimators. It is important to note that the theory developed here is independent of the specific design of variance-reduction based selection rule introduced in Section
6
and applies to general ER-based off-policy policy optimization methods.

5.1
Replaying Old Samples with Dependence Cause Policy Gradient Bias

It has been empirically observed that given a fixed replay capacity, the performance of ER-based RL algorithms tends to improve as the age of the oldest policy decreases
(
Fedus et al. 2020
)
. The theoretical study presented in this section offers insights into this observation.

Definition 1

(
Fedus et al. 2020
)
.

The
age
of a historical sample and associated policy
in the ER is defined to be the number of iterations passed
since the sample
was generated.

Despite their popularity, Policy Gradient (PG) methods have significant challenges in generating unbiased policy gradient estimators in the infinite-horizon MDP optimization. A critical condition for unbiased estimators, as outlined by the Policy Gradient Theorem
(
Sutton et al. 1999b
)
, requires the sampling of state-action pairs
(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})
must be drawn from the stationary distribution of the Markov chain under the candidate policy of interest. However, as highlighted by
Zhang et al. 2020
, many PG algorithms sequentially generate samples and the
dependence
induced by Markovian noise consequently introduces bias into the policy gradient estimators. Furthermore, due to the mechanism of ER, the sample reuse will further increase the dependence between behavior policies, affecting the bias in policy gradient estimators.
To summarize, we present the upper bound on the bias of both LR and CLR policy gradient estimators in Theorem
3
. The proof is provided in Appendix
D.1
.

At any
k
-th iteration, when the historical samples
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)}=({\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i,j)},{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i,j)})
with
j=1,2,\ldots,n
generated in the
i
-th iteration under
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}
are reused for the policy gradient estimation, we use the lag term
t
to control the impact of dependence induced by Markovian noise and derive an upper bound on the bias of the LR and CLR gradient estimators (
15
) and (
16
) in Theorem
3
that is applicable to
any positive integer
t<i
. However, it’s important to note that the bound becomes tighter as the iteration
k
increases and the learning rate
\eta_{k}
decreases when we choose the mixing time defined below, i.e.,
t=t_{k}^{\star}
.

Definition 2

(Mixing Time,
Wu et al. 2020
)
.

Given the learning rate
\eta_{k}
, the mixing time of an ergodic MDP with a fixed policy
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}
is defined as

t_{k}^{\star}\vcentcolon=\min\{t\geq 0|\varphi(nt)\leq\eta_{k}\}
.

Theorem 3

(Gradient Biasedness)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
A.1
and
A.2
hold. For LR/CLR policy gradient estimators in Eq. (
15
) and (
16
), we consider the policy update rule (
2
) with the learning rate
\eta_{k}
. For any integer
t
such that
0\leq t<i
with
{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}
, the bias of policy gradient estimators (
R\in
{
LR,CLR
}
) can be bounded by

\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})]\right\|\leq\frac{Z_{1}^{R}(t+1)}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{i}_{\ell=i-t}\eta_{\ell}+2M\varphi(nt)+\frac{Z_{2}^{R}}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{k}_{\ell=i-t}\eta_{\ell}\\

where
Z_{1}^{LR}=2nM^{2}U_{\pi}
,
Z_{1}^{CLR}=4nM^{2}U_{\pi}U_{L}U_{f}
,
Z_{2}^{LR}=M(2MC_{d}+ML_{g}+U_{L}L_{g}+MU_{\pi})
and
Z_{2}^{CLR}=M(2MC_{d}+ML_{g}+U_{f}L_{g}+MU_{\pi})
. Here,
C_{d}
and
L_{g}
are defined in Lemma
4
and Lemma
7
respectively in Appendix
C.3
.

Proof.

(
Sketch
)
In the proof sketch, we focus on LR policy gradient estimator (
15
); but the CLR estimator (
16
) shares similar proof steps. The bias of the gradient estimator is,

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla J}^{LR}_{k}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})]\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|n}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{n}_{j=1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}\right)}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}\right)}g\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|n}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{n}_{j=1}\left\|\Delta_{\textbf{(i)}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\|

(18)

where
\Delta_{\textbf{(i)}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\vcentcolon={\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}\right)}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}\right)}g\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]
.
To separately quantify the impact induced by policy update, sample reuse, and Markovian noise,
\Delta_{\textbf{(i)}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})

is decomposed below based on triangle inequality theorem, i.e.,

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta_{\textbf{(i)}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla J}^{LR}\left({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)-\widehat{\nabla J}^{LR}\left({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)\right]\right\|

(19)

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla J}^{LR}\left({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)-\widehat{\nabla J}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)\right]\right\|

(20)

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla J}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)-\widehat{\nabla J}\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)\right]\right\|

(21)

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla J}\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)-\widehat{\nabla J}\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)\right]\right\|

(22)

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla J}\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)-\nabla J\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)\right]\right\|.

(23)

The notations above are defined in Appendix
C.2
. In brief,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)}=(\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i,j)},\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i,j)})
represents a
j
-th state-action pair collected in iteration
i
. It is generated starting
from the state
\boldsymbol{s}^{(i-t,j)}
by following a
fixed policy

\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}
with
0\leq t<i
and the corresponding sequence from
\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i-t,j)}
to
\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)}
is called auxiliary Markov chain (AMC). In addition,
\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)}=(\check{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i,j)},\check{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i,j)})\sim d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}(\cdot,\cdot)
represents a state-action pair
independently
generated from the stationary distribution with a fixed policy
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}
and the corresponding sequence from timestep
(i-t,j)
to
(i,j)
is called the stationary Markov chain (SMC). See their formal definitions in Appendix
C.1
.

By Lipschitz conditions on policy and gradient updates, i.e.,
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)})-\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)})

and
g\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)-g\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)
over
{\mathbb{E}}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\|
, we can bound the terms (
19
) and (
22
) with
\mathcal{O}(\sum^{k}_{\ell=i-t}\eta_{\ell})
by applying Lemmas
9
and
12
.
Further, by applying Lemma
10
, the term (
20
) is bounded by the total variation
\left\|\mathbb{P}\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\in\cdot|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i-t,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)-\mathbb{P}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i,j)}\in\cdot|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i-t,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)\right\|_{TV}
in terms of
\mathcal{O}(t\sum^{i}_{\ell=i-t}\eta_{\ell})
. Basically, to account for the sequential policy updates from
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}
to
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}
,
this is achieved by recursively bounding total variation
between
\mathbb{P}\left({\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i-t,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)

and
\mathbb{P}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i-t,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)

by using the telescoping sum of the total variations from the timestep
(i-t,j)
to the timestep
(i,j)
. Then by applying Lemma
11
to account for the dependence impact induced by Markovian noise, the term (
21
) is bounded by the total variation distance between the MC state transition probability
\mathbb{P}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i,j)}\in\cdot|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i-t,j)};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)
and the independent stationary distribution
d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}(\cdot)
following the uniform ergodicity assumption (
A.2
), which leads to
\mathcal{O}(\varphi(nt))
.
The last term (
23
) can be bounded by the total variation between two stationary distributions
d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}}(\cdot)
and
d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-1}}}(\cdot)
. By applying Lemma
13
, the difference is associated to policy update

{\mathbb{E}}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\|
and bounded by
\mathcal{O}(\sum^{k}_{\ell=i-t}\eta_{\ell})
.

Therefore, we can bound the bias of the single-sample LR gradient estimator,

\left\|\Delta_{\textbf{(i)}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\|\leq 2M\varphi(nt)+2nM^{2}U_{\pi}t\sum^{i}_{\ell=i-t}\eta_{\ell}+M(2MC_{d}+L_{g}M+U_{L}L_{g}+MU_{\pi})\sum^{k}_{\ell=i-t}\eta_{\ell}.

from which the conclusion follows. The detailed proof is provided in Appendix
D.1
and
G
.
∎

Then by applying Theorem
3
and setting
i=k
(without sample reuse), we immediately obtain the upper bound for the bias of the classical PG estimator (
5
) in Corollary
1
.

Corollary 1
.

Suppose that Assumptions
A.1
and
A.2
hold.
For any integer
t
such that
0\leq t<i
, the bias of policy gradient estimator (
5
) can be bounded by

\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})]\right\|\leq{Z_{1}^{LR}(t+1)}\sum^{k}_{\ell=k-t}\eta_{\ell}+2M\varphi(nt)+{Z_{2}^{LR}}\sum^{k}_{\ell=k-t}\eta_{\ell}

where
Z_{1}^{LR}
and
Z_{1}^{LR}
are specified in Theorem
3
.

Consider a decreasing learning rate
\eta_{k}=\eta_{1}k^{-r}
where
\eta_{1}
and
r\in(0,1)
are positive constant. Notice that
\sum^{i}_{i-t}\eta_{\ell}\leq t\eta_{i-t}
and
\sum^{k}_{\ell=i-t}\eta_{\ell}\leq(k-i+t)\eta_{i-t}
due to
k\geq i>t
. For
R\in\{LR,CLR\}
The bound in Theorem
3
can be expressed in
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
notation

\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})]\right\|\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\frac{(t+1)t}{(i-t)^{r}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\frac{k-i+t}{(i-t)^{r}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\varphi(nt)\right).

(24)

This upper bound shows that the bias of LR/CLR policy gradient estimator becomes larger when samples from older policies (i.e., smaller value of
i
) are selected since both terms
\frac{1}{(i-t)^{r}}
and
\frac{k-i+t}{(i-t)^{r}}
increase in
i
for a fixed iteration
k
. Also, by observing the fact
k-i\leq B_{k}
where the buffer size
B_{k}
is a function of
k
, the bound (
24
) can be expressed in the form of

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})]\right\|\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{(t+1)t}{(k-B_{k}-t)^{r}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{B_{k}+t}{(k-B_{k}-t)^{r}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\varphi(nt)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\frac{r^{2}}{n^{2}}\log_{\kappa}^{2}k}{(k-B_{k}+\frac{r}{n}\log_{\kappa}k)^{r}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{B_{k}-\frac{r}{n}\log_{\kappa}k}{(k-B_{k}+\frac{r}{n}\log_{\kappa}k)^{r}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{k^{r}}\right)

(25)

where the mixing rate of the process
\varphi(nt)
becomes
\mathcal{O}(k^{-r})
if the lag term
t
is chosen to be the mixing time
t_{k}^{*}=\log_{\kappa}k^{-r/n}
.
Therefore, we have several theoretical conclusions:

•

Asymptotically Unbiased
. The bias converges in norm
\|{\mathbb{E}}[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}]-{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})]\|\rightarrow 0
as
k\rightarrow\infty
if
B_{k}=\mathcal{O}(k^{r})
, indicating asymptotic unbiasedness of estimators (
15
) and (
16
). One special case satisfying this condition is that the buffer size
B_{k}
is constant.

•

Replaying Old Samples Causes High Bias
. If the buffer size increases at a rate higher than
\mathcal{O}(k^{r})
, the LR/CLR policy gradient estimators might have an unbounded bias. This bias tends to be minimized if no experience is replayed, i.e.,
\mathcal{U}_{k}=\{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\}
. In such case, the bias is bounded by
\mathcal{O}\left({(t+1)t}{(k-t)^{-r}}\right)
by applying (
24
).

•

Mixing Condition
. The convergence rate of the bias is closely connected to the mixing rate of the environment. A higher mixing rate leads to a faster convergence.

5.2
Sample Correlation Increase Gradient Variance

At any
k
-th iteration, given a reuse set
|\mathcal{U}|_{k}
, the total variance of the LR/CLR policy gradient estimators (
15
) and (
16
) is shown in Proposition
1
; see the proof in Appendix
D.2
.

Proposition 1

(Gradient Variance)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
A.1
and
A.2
hold. At the
k
-th iteration with the target policy
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}
, given the reuse set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
that includes behavior policies, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
, the total variance of LR/CLR policy gradient estimators (
R\in
{
LR,CLR
}
) in Eq. (
15
) and (
16
) can be decomposed to

{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]\right)=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{R}_{i,k}\right)^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{R}_{i^{\prime},k}\right)

(26)

where
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{R}_{i,k}=\left(\sqrt{{\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R,(1)}_{i,k}\right]},\sqrt{{\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R,(2)}_{i,k}\right]},\ldots,\sqrt{{\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R,(d)}_{i,k}\right]}\right)^{\top}
and

\mathbf{P}_{i,i^{\prime},k}={\rm Diag}\left({\rm Corr}^{(1)}_{i,i^{\prime},k},\ldots,{\rm Corr}^{(d)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right)
with correlation coefficients of gradient estimate pairs
{\rm Corr}_{i,i^{\prime},k}^{(\ell)}={\rm Corr}\left(\widehat{\nabla}J^{R,(\ell)}_{i,k},\widehat{\nabla}J^{R,(\ell)}_{i^{\prime},k}\right)
for each
\ell
-th dimension of policy parameters with
\ell=1,2,\ldots,d
. By using the greatest element of
\mathbf{P}
, the total variance (
26
) is bounded by

{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]\right)\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\max_{\ell=1,2,\ldots,d}\left({\rm Corr}^{(\ell)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{R}_{i,k}\right)^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{R}_{i^{\prime},k}\right).

(27)

Proposition
1
implies that replaying past experiences or historical samples can reduce the variance of LR/CLR policy gradient estimators in (
15
) and (
16
), provided the correlations between individual LR/CLR policy gradient estimators (i.e.,
\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{i,k}
and
\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{i^{\prime},k}
with
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}
included in the reuse set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
) are less than 1.
Theorem
3
and Proposition
1
suggest a trade-off in experience replay:
while historical sample reusing reduces the variance of LR/CLR policy gradient estimators, it concurrently increases their bias.
On one hand, replaying less dependent old experiences is desirable; on the other hand, it’s necessary to discard too old behavior policies and associated historical samples to prevent bias escalation. This observation underscores the critical role of selecting reuse set and buffer size in achieving an optimal balance between variance reduction and bias control in policy gradient estimation.
To reduce the variance, an experience selection method will be proposed in Section
6
. In addition,
the effect of buffer size and experience selection on the policy optimization convergence will be further studied theoretically
in Section
7
and empirically in Section
8
.

6
Variance Reduction Experience Replay for Policy Optimization

Before one can use the IS based policy gradient estimators developed in Section
4
, there is one key remaining question:
How to select the behavior policies, among all historical candidate policies and samples visited up to the
k
-th iteration, to be included in the reuse set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
?

Instead of reusing all historical samples, reusing only the most relevant ones can reduce computational cost, stabilize likelihood ratio calculations, and reduce the gradient estimation variance.
In short, a well-designed selection rule can improve sample efficiency on policy gradient estimation and
accelerate the learning of optimal policy.

In Section
6.1
, we propose a selection criterion to construct the reuse set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
so that it can reduce the variance of LR/CLR policy gradient estimators (
15
) and (
16
).
To support the selection, in Section
6.2
, we provide the variance estimation for PG and individual LR/CLR policy gradient estimators required by the criterion.
Since the proposed selection criterion needs to assess all historical samples and associated behavior policies
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
to determine
\mathcal{U}_{k}
, the likelihood ratio calculations can be computationally expensive as the size of
\mathcal{F}_{k}
increases.
Thus, we derive a computationally efficient approximated selection rule in Section
6.3
.
Based on the proposed selection criteria, we develop a
variance reduction experience replay (VRER) approach, as well as
VRER based generic policy gradient optimization algorithm (called PG-VRER), in Section
6.4
that is applicable to the state-of-the-art (SOTA) policy optimization approaches and can enhance their performance.

6.1
Selection Rule for Reducing Policy Gradient Estimation Variance

To improve the policy gradient estimation, we want to select the reuse set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
producing stable likelihood ratios and thus reducing the estimation variance of policy gradient.

Even though the target distribution can be high dimensional for complex systems with high uncertainty, the proposed VRER based policy gradient estimators
tend to intelligently put more weight on historical observations that are most likely sampled from the target distribution.

Motivated by this insight, we propose a selection criterion in Theorem
4
based on the comparison of variances of the classical PG estimator (
5
) and the individual LR/CLR estimators (
14
) and (
17
)
to dynamically determine the reuse set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
for each
k
-th iteration.
The variance difference between
\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR}_{i,k}
and
\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}

is induced by the discrepancy between the behavior and target policies, i.e.,
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}
and
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}
.
If
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}
, the likelihood ratio
f_{i,k}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})=\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})}
in (
14
) equals to 1 so
\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR}_{i,k}=\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}
and they have the same variance.
Therefore, the proposed selection criterion (
28
) puts a constant threshold
c
on the variance inflation of individual LR/CLR policy gradient estimator
\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{i,k}
(with
R\in
{LR,CLR}) compared to
\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}
.
To ensure that new samples generated in the current
k
-th iteration are always included in the set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
, we consider the constant
c>1
. In addition, Theorem
4
demonstrates that the selection criterion (
28
) guides appropriate reuse of historical samples and leads to variance reduction of the average LR/CLR policy gradient estimators in (
15
) and (
16
) compared to the classical PG estimator by a factor of
c/|\mathcal{U}_{k}|
; see
(
29
).
The proof of Theorem
4
is provided in Appendix
D.3
.

Theorem 4
.

At the
k
-th iteration with the target distribution
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}
, the reuse set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
is created to include the behavioral distributions, i.e.,
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}
with
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
, whose total variance of individual LR/CLR policy gradient estimators in (
14
) and
(
17
) is no greater than
c
times the total variance of the classical PG estimator for some constant
c>1
. Mathematically, for
R\in
{
LR,CLR
}
,

\text{{Selection Rule 1:}}~~~{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{i,k}\right]\right)\leq c{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\right]\right).

(28)

Then, based on such reuse set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
, the total variances of the average LR/CLR policy gradient estimators (
15
) and (
16
) are no greater than the total variance of the PG estimator scaled by the averaged max correlation between individual LR/CLR policy gradient estimates,

{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]\right)\leq\frac{c}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\right]\right)\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\max_{\ell=1,2,\ldots,d}\left({\rm Corr}^{(\ell)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right)

(29)

Moreover, it holds that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\frac{c}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\max_{\ell=1,2,\ldots,d}\left({\rm Corr}^{(\ell)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right){\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+2\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]\right\|^{2}+2\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})]\right\|^{2}.

6.2
Variance Estimation for Policy Gradient Estimators

When the selection criterion in (
28
) is used determine the reuse set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
, we need to estimate the variances of the PG estimator (
5
) and the individual LR/CLR estimators in (
14
) and (
17
). One key problem is the dependence structure of scenario-based sample gradients. To address it, the Moving Block Bootstrap (MBB) technique
(
Kunsch 1989
;
Liu et al. 1992
)
is utilized.
Unlike traditional bootstrap methods that resample individual observations, MBB uses a strategy of resampling blocks of consecutive observations so that
the dependence structure inherent in the original dataset is preserved in each block.

Let
g_{i,j}\vcentcolon=\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)}g\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)
denote the scenario-based LR policy gradient
observation and suppose
l\in[1,n]
is an integer. Let
X_{j}=(g_{i,j},\ldots,g_{i,j+l-1})
denote the block of length
l
starting with
g_{i,j}
with
1\leq j\leq N
where
N=n-l+1
. To obtain the MBB samples, we randomly select a suitable number of blocks from
the collection
\{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{N}\}
. Accordingly, let
\{X_{1}^{*},X_{2}^{*},\ldots,X_{N}^{*}\}
denote a bootstrapped sample through drawing with replacement from
\{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{N}\}
. Let
\bar{g}_{i,j}=(g_{i,j}+g_{i,j+1}+\ldots+g_{i,j+l-1})/l
denote the average of the block. Let
\bar{g}_{i}=N^{-1}\sum^{N}_{j=1}\bar{g}_{i,j}
. Then, the conditional variance estimate of individual LR estimator (
14
)
(
Lahiri 2003
, Chapter 3.2.1)

and its trace are

\widehat{{\rm Var}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR}_{i,k}\right]=\frac{l}{n}\left[\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{j=1}\bar{g}_{i,j}\bar{g}_{i,j}^{\top}-\bar{g}_{i}\bar{g}_{i}^{\top}\right]\mbox{ and }{\rm Tr}\left(\widehat{{\rm Var}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR}_{i,k}\right]\right)=\frac{l}{n}\left[\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{j=1}\|\bar{g}_{i,j}\|^{2}-\|\bar{g}_{i}\|^{2}\right].

(30)

This approach can be applied to the individual CLR estimator (
17
) by clipping the likelihood ratios in
g_{i,j}
.
Similarly, the variance of the PG estimator (
5
) and its trace are

\widehat{{\rm Var}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\right]=\frac{l}{n}\left[\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{j=1}\bar{g}_{k,j}\bar{g}_{k,j}^{\top}-\bar{g}_{k}\bar{g}_{k}^{\top}\right]\mbox{ and }{\rm Tr}\left(\widehat{{\rm Var}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\right]\right)=\frac{l}{n}\left[\frac{1}{N}\sum^{N}_{j=1}\|\bar{g}_{k,j}\|^{2}-\|\bar{g}_{k}\|^{2}\right].

(31)

The consistency of the MBB estimators has been rigorously established.
Lahiri 2003

demonstrates that under mild conditions of the moment and strong mixing of the stochastic process
\{g_{i,j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
, the MBB variance estimators (
30
) and (
31
) are consistent across a broad spectrum of bootstrap block sizes
l
. This consistency holds as long as
l
increases to infinity alongside the sample size
n
, but does so at a rate that is slower than
n
. This finding shows the effectiveness of the MBB approach in accurately estimating variance; see Appendix
E
for the verification of the regularity conditions.

Remark 1

(Bias Reduction)
.

The correlation in state transitions
\{(\boldsymbol{s}_{t},\boldsymbol{a}_{t})\}_{t\geq 1}
not only affects the estimation variance but also introduces bias in the gradient estimators. Consequently, when applying the variance-based selection rule (
28
) for step-based algorithms, it’s important to mitigate this dependence to further reduce the estimation bias. To achieve this, we utilize
downsampling strategy
, i.e., draw with replacement
n_{0}
(with
n_{0}<n
) samples from selected historical observations under policy
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}
; see Step 2(d) of Algorithm
1
.

6.3
Gradient Variance Ratio Approximation

The selection rule (
28
) could be computationally expensive for complex systems, especially as the historical set
|\mathcal{F}_{k}|
becomes large since it requires the repeated calculation of the bootstrapped variances of individual LR policy gradient estimator in (
14
) for each
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
.
To support real-time decision making, we provide second-order Taylor
approximations to the policy gradient variance ratio,

{\rm Tr}({\rm Var}[\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR}_{i,k}])/{\rm Tr}({\rm Var}[\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}])
for step-based algorithms.

To develop an efficient selection rule, we now turn to a more adaptable scenario: Instead of iteration-based selection, we scan historical samples individually in the buffer and select those that are likely to exhibit lower gradient variance. By focusing on individual samples, we have the flexibility to adjust the number of steps per iteration to
n=1
. We then provide the approximation of selection rule (
28
) in Proposition
2
by using second-order Taylor expansion. The proof is given in Appendix
F
. A similar approach can be applied to the individual CLR policy gradient estimator in (
17
) by clipping all likelihood ratios in the proof and the conclusion in Proposition
2
holds for the CLR estimator by replacing the KL divergence term in (
32
) with
{\mathbb{E}}[\min(\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})},U_{f})]
.

Proposition 2
.

Under Assumptions
A.1
and
A.2
, for any
t
such that
t<i\leq k
, if
t=o(k^{r/2})
and the buffer size
B_{k}=o(k^{r/2})
, the total variance ratio of the individual LR policy gradient estimator and PG estimator has the approximation

\frac{{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR}_{i,k}\right]\right)}{{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\right]\right)}\approx e^{{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\rm{KL}}\left(\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}(\cdot|\boldsymbol{s})\|\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}(\cdot|\boldsymbol{s})\right)\right]}\left(1+\zeta_{k}^{-1}\right)-\zeta^{-1}_{k}

(32)

where
\zeta_{k}={{\rm Tr}({\rm Var}[\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}])}/{\|{\mathbb{E}}[\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}]\|^{2}}
is the relative variance.

To avoid the intensive computation, the moving averages of stochastic gradients and their element-wise square from Adam optimizer
(
Kingma and Ba 2015
)
to first and second moment of policy gradient are used, i.e.,

\displaystyle\tilde{m}_{k}

\displaystyle=\beta_{1}\tilde{m}_{k-1}+(1-\beta_{1})g_{k}

\displaystyle m_{k}=\frac{\tilde{m}_{k}}{1-\beta_{1}^{k+1}}

\displaystyle\tilde{v}_{k}

\displaystyle=\beta_{2}\tilde{v}_{k-1}+(1-\beta_{2})g_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle v_{k}=\frac{\tilde{v}_{k}}{1-\beta_{2}^{k+1}}

with
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in(0,1)
and
g_{k}
is the gradient estimate at iteration
k
. Based on the insights presented in
Balles and Hennig 2018
, the term
v_{k}-m_{k}^{2}
can be seen as an estimate of the stochastic gradient variances and
\hat{\zeta}_{k}=(v_{k}-m_{k}^{2})/m_{k}^{2}
is an estimate of the relative variance. Utilizing the first and second moment estimators from the Adam optimizer eliminates the need for additional computational resources to estimate gradient variance in
\zeta_{k}
. The only computational demand arises from calculating the KL divergence between policies.

Then by plugging the relative variance estimates
\zeta_{k}\approx\hat{\zeta}_{k}
in the approximation (
32
), after rearranging both sides, the selection rule (
28
) becomes

\mbox{{Selection Rule 2:}}~~~{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mbox{KL}\left(\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}(\cdot|\boldsymbol{s})\|\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}(\cdot|\boldsymbol{s})\right)\right]\leq\log\left(1+(c-1)\frac{\hat{\zeta}_{k}}{\hat{\zeta}_{k}+1}\right).

(33)

This new selection rule can be viewed as a dynamic threshold of the distance between behavior and target policies depending on the relative variance
\hat{\zeta}_{k}
. When
c=1
, it becomes a very conservative bound that only selects samples generated from the target policy
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}
.

6.4
VRER Assisted Generic Policy Optimization Algorithm

This section presents the generic procedure of variance reduction experience replay based policy optimization, called
PG-VRER
, in Algorithm
1
. At
k
-th iteration, we generate
n
new samples, denoted by
\mathcal{T}_{k}
, by following the candidate policy
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}
and update the data set
\mathcal{D}_{k}
in Step 1. Next, we select the historical samples satisfying the selection rule (
28
) or (
33
) and
create the reuse set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
in Step 2. Then, we proceed to optimize the objective function
J(\boldsymbol{\theta})
over
K_{off}
epochs using mini-batch gradient ascent,
as outlined in Step 3. In this context, at each
h
-th iteration of offline policy optimization, we utilize randomly selected samples, labeled as
\mathcal{D}^{n_{0}}_{k}
, to generate a mini-batch for stochastic gradient updates
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}^{h+1}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}^{h}+\eta_{k}\widehat{\nabla}J_{k}^{h}
. After the off-line optimization, we update the set
\mathcal{F}_{k+1}
through a buffer with size
B
, i.e., removing the oldest policy
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1-B}
and inserting the latest one
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1}
in Step 4. We repeat this procedure until reaching to the iteration budget, denoted by
K
.

Input
: the selection threshold constant
c
; the maximum number of iterations
K
; buffer size
B
; the number of replications per iteration (batch size)
n
; the number of iterations in offline optimization
K_{off}
;
the set of historical samples
\mathcal{D}_{0}
; the set of policy parameters visited so far
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.

Initialize
policy
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}
and set
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}^{0}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}
. Then store the parameter, i.e.,
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\mathcal{F}_{0}\cup\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}\right\}
;

for

k=1,2,\ldots,K

do

1.
Execution:

(a) Generate
n
new samples by following
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{T}_{k}\vcentcolon=\left\{\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{(k,j)},\boldsymbol{a}^{(k,j)}\right);j=1,\ldots,n\right\}
;

(b) Store them
\mathcal{D}_{k}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{k-1}\cup\mathcal{T}_{k}
;

2.
Selection:

(a) Initialize
\mathcal{U}_{k}=\emptyset
and the set
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{n_{0}}=\emptyset
;

for

\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}

do

(b) Verify the selection criterion (
28
) or (
33
);

if

Selection Criterion is Satisfied

then

(c) Update the reuse set:
\mathcal{U}_{k}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}_{k}\cup\{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\}.

(d) Randomly sample
n_{0}
observations from
\mathcal{T}_{i}
:

\mathcal{T}_{i}^{n_{0}}=\left\{\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}\right)\sim\mathcal{T}_{i}:j=1,2\ldots,n_{0}\right\}

and store them to the set
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{n_{0}}=\mathcal{D}_{k}^{n_{0}}\cup\mathcal{T}_{i}^{n_{0}}
; end if

end for

3.
Offline Optimization (Algorithm-Specific):

for

h=0,...,K_{off}-1

do

(a) Sample a mini-batch from
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{n_{0}}
to compute the policy gradient estimates
\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR,h}_{k}
or
\widehat{\nabla}J^{CLR,h}_{k}
defined in Equations (
15
) and (
16
);

(b) Update the policy parameters by

\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}^{h+1}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}^{h}+\eta_{k}\widehat{\nabla}J_{k}^{h}

using the gradient estimate
\widehat{\nabla}J_{k}^{h}
from Step 3(a).

end for

4. Set
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}^{K_{off}}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1}^{0}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1}
. Store it to the set
\mathcal{F}_{k+1}=\mathcal{F}_{k}\cup\{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1}\}
. If
|\mathcal{F}_{k+1}|>B
, remove
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1-B}
from
\mathcal{F}_{k+1}
.

end for

Algorithm 1

VRER Assisted Generic Policy Gradient Algorithm (
PG-VRER
)

The proposed VRER method is inherently generic and applies to a wide range of policy gradient optimization algorithms. The implementation details of the Execution (Step 1) and Offline Optimization (Step 3) steps may vary depending on the specific policy gradient algorithm in use.
For the execution step, it is possible to collect samples in parallel from multiple environments, and for the offline optimization step, the epoch
K_{off}
may vary between actor updates and critic updates for certain actor-critic style algorithms.

The selection rule (
28
) can be computationally expensive due to the fact that
it needs to repeatedly calculate the bootstrapped variance of policy gradient estimators. In contrast, the approximation in (
33
) only requires computing the KL divergence between target and behavior policies, which can be performed efficiently for commonly used policy functions such as Gaussian policies and softmax policies. Additionally, the gradient variance
{\rm Var}[\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}]
in (
33
) can be obtained from Adam optimizer
(
Kingma and Ba 2015
)
without introducing extra computational cost.

Unlike the episode-based approaches, the samples of step-based approaches are collected sequentially, and thus are often dependent.
This impacts policy learning and gradient variance estimation. To reduce the correlation impact,
we randomly draw
n_{0}
samples from historical observations following each selected policy, i.e.,
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{n_{0}}=\bigcup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\mathcal{T}_{i}^{n_{0}}=\bigcup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\left\{\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)}\sim\mathcal{T}_{i}:j=1,2,\ldots,n_{0}\right\}
.

Remark 2

(Buffer Size)
.

The
replay buffer size
, specified by
B
, can be fixed or dynamic. An example of a dynamic approach is setting the buffer size to be proportional to the number of iterations, such as selecting the
B=k\xi
most recent samples, where
\xi
represents the ratio of the total number of reusable iterations to the overall iterations.

There is always a trade-off on specifying the replay buffer size to balance exploration and exploitation.

As
Fedus et al. 2020
pointed out, there is an interplay between: (1)
the improvements caused by increasing the replay capacity and covering large state-action space;
and (2) the deterioration caused by having older policies in the
buffer or overfitting to some “out-dated"
samples. As discussed in Section
5
, this interplay also creates a bias-variance trade-off: increasing the replay capacity reduces the policy gradient estimation variance but increases the bias.
The magnitude of both effects depends on the particular settings of these quantities.
We will delve into the theoretical analysis of buffer size in Section
7
and explore its empirical impact in Section
8
.

Remark 3
.

The importance sampling approach, used to leverage the information from historical samples, requires sampling distributions to be independent with each other.
The interdependencies between historical samples can lead to an obstacle for both the stability and convergence of policy optimization. To reduce this interdependence, we utilize
randomly sampling strategy
, i.e., draw with replacement
n_{0}
(with
n_{0}<n
) samples from selected historical observations with
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}
in Step 2(d), and then use them to train the off-line policy optimization in Step 3 through mini-batch stochastic gradient ascent.

7
Finite-Time Convergence Analysis of PG-VRER

In this section, our attention turns to a thorough theoretical analysis of the proposed experience replay and optimal policy learning method, i.e., PG-VRER.
We establish its asymptotic and finite-time performances as well as the buffer size management, specifically designed to enhance the convergence rate.
