Finite-time convergence analysis within the context of experience replay introduces substantial challenges stemming from the intricate interplay between sample dependence induced by Markovian noise in dynamics and behavior policy interdependence. The former concern predominantly affects step-based algorithms and leads to a bias in policy gradient estimation, as discussed in Section
4.1
. The latter challenge extends its complexity to both episode-based and step-based algorithms, rendering many conventional proof techniques ineffective. To address these two issues, we apply the theoretical tools developed in Section
5
to investigate the finite-time performance of the PG-VRER algorithm proposed in Section
6
.

We present the finite-time convergence analysis in Theorem
5
for the proposed PG-VRER algorithm. This finite-time analysis shows that the proposed VRER with the selection rule (
28
) can improve rate of convergence compared with the PG estimator
in (
5
).
Following the literature of non-convex optimization
(
Zhang et al. 2019
;
Zhou and Cong 2018
)
,
we choose to state the convergence in terms of the average over all
K
iterations. This theorem presents the effect of the learning rate
\eta_{k}
and mixing rate
\varphi(nt)
on the average gradient norm, as well as the effect of the buffer size
B_{K}
.
See the proof in Appendix
D.4
.

Theorem 5

(Convergence of PG-VRER)
.

Suppose Assumptions
A.1
and
A.2
hold. Let
\eta_{k}=\eta_{1}k^{-r}
denote the learning rate used in the
k
-th iteration with two constants
\eta_{1}\in(0,\frac{1}{4L}]
and
r\in(0,1)
. By running Algorithm
1
(with the replay buffer of size
B_{K}
), for both LR and CLR policy gradient estimators in Eq. (
15
) and (
16
) and
t\leq K-B_{K}
, we have the rate of convergence

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K}\sum^{K}_{k=1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8U_{J}/\eta_{1}}{K^{1-r}}+\frac{4cLM^{2}}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\eta_{k}}\bar{\rho}_{k}+{4M^{2}}\varphi(nt)+\frac{2^{r+1}C_{3}}{(1-r)K^{r}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2^{r+1}C_{2}\eta_{1}(t+1)t}{(1-r)K^{r}}+\frac{2^{r+1}C_{1}\eta_{1}}{1-r}\frac{B_{K}+t}{K^{r}}+M^{2}\frac{B_{K}+t}{K}

where
C_{1}=\max\{C^{\Gamma}_{1},C^{\Gamma}_{2}\}
,
C_{2}={4nM^{3}U_{\pi}U_{L}U_{f}}
,
C_{3}=\sup_{k\geq 1}\|{\rm Bias}_{k}\|
with
{\rm Bias}_{k}={\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\left[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]
and
\bar{\rho}_{k}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\left|\max_{\ell=1,2,\ldots,d}\left({\rm Corr}^{(\ell)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right)\right|
. Here
R\in\{LR,CLR\},

C_{1}^{\Gamma}
and
C_{2}^{\Gamma}
are defined in Lemma
14
,
and
c
is the selection constant defined in Theorem
4
. Using
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
notation gives

\frac{1}{K}\sum^{K}_{k=1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\|^{2}\right]=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{K^{1-r}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\eta_{k}}\bar{\rho}_{k}}{K}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\varphi(nt)\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{t^{2}}{K^{r}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{B_{K}+t}{K^{r}}\right)

(34)

where
n
is the number of steps in each iteration. The notation

\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
hides constants
c
,
L
,
\eta_{1}
,
M
,
U_{J}
,
n
,
U_{f}
,
U_{\pi}
,
U_{L}
,
\kappa_{0}
,
\kappa
and
L_{g}
.

Theorem
5
guarantees the local convergence of the proposed PG-VRER algorithm if LR or CLR policy gradient estimators are used in policy updates. It can be seen that the rate of optimal convergence depends on three key factors: (1) sample correlation
\bar{\rho}_{k}
; (2) mixing rate
\varphi(nt)=\kappa_{0}\kappa^{nt}
; and (3) buffer size
B_{K}
. In short, low sample correlations between reused samples and a faster mixing rate of the environment (i.e., smaller
\kappa
) would improve the convergence.

Since
\bar{\rho}_{k}\leq 1
, when the learning rate
\eta_{k}=\eta_{1}k^{-r}
with
\eta_{1}\in(0,\frac{1}{4L}]
and
r\in(0,1)
, the second term of Eq. (
34
) can be simplified to

\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\eta_{k}}\bar{\rho}_{k}\leq\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\eta_{k}}\leq\frac{1}{1-r}K^{-r}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{K^{r}}\right).

Therefore, Corollary
2
can be obtained immediately.

Corollary 2
.

Suppose Assumptions
A.1
and
A.2
hold. Under the same configurations as Theorem
5
, we have the rate of convergence

\frac{1}{K}\sum^{K}_{k=1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\|^{2}\right]=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{K^{1-r}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{K^{r}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\varphi(nt)\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{t^{2}}{K^{r}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{B_{K}+t}{K^{r}}\right).

In our detailed analysis of the convergence rate outlined in Theorem
5
, it can be observed that the VRER contributes to the stabilization of the gradient, as evidenced by the term
\bar{\rho}_{k}
. Nonetheless, based on the insights from Corollary
2
, this impact, while notable, does not constitute a limiting factor in the rate of convergence, when compared to the effect of sample-dependence, including mixing rate
\mathcal{O}(\varphi(nt))
and gradient bias terms
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{t^{2}}{K^{r}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{B_{K}+t}{K^{r}}\right)
.

Corollary 3
.

Suppose Assumptions
A.1
and
A.2
hold. Under the same configurations as Theorem
5
, by setting
t=t^{\star}_{K}=\log_{\kappa}K^{-r/n}
, we have the rate of convergence

\frac{1}{K}\sum^{K}_{k=1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\|^{2}\right]\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{K^{1-r}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{(t_{K}^{\star})^{2}}{K^{r}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{B_{K}+t_{K}^{\star}}{K^{r}}\right)

where the notation

\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
hides constants
c
,
L
,
\eta_{1}
,
M
,
U_{J}
,
n
,
U_{f}
,
U_{\pi}
,
U_{L}
,
\kappa_{0}
,
\kappa
and
L_{g}
.

Corollary
3
establishes a convergence rate for the average squared norm of the policy gradient for the PG-VRER algorithm when the lag term equals the mixing time
t=t_{K}^{\star}
. Its proof can be found in Appendix
D.5
. By letting

q=\min\{1-r,r\}
, the
convergence rate
can be expressed as
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{B_{K}+(t_{K}^{\star})^{2}}{K^{q}}\right)
influenced by both the buffer size
B_{K}
and a term related to the mixing time
t_{K}^{\star}
. Consistent with the conclusion in gradient bias analysis (Theorem
3
), Corollary
3
indicates that the convergence is only guaranteed when the buffer size scales at a rate lower than
\mathcal{O}(K^{r})
, acknowledging that replaying old samples introduces extra bias.

8
Empirical Study

We perform most of our experiments using the Gym
(
Brockman et al. 2016
)
and PyBullet
(
Ellenberger 2018–2019
)
, which provide simulators for various classical control tasks. Both are commonly used benchmark environments for RL algorithms. Our goal is to assess the finite sample performance of the proposed PG-VRER with three
state-of-the-art (SOTA) policy optimization approaches and conduct thorough stability and sensitivity analyses. In our experiments, we used the simplified selection rule (
33
) to efficiently select historical samples and construct the reuse set. Trajectories in the figures were depicted with mean and 95% confidence interval (c.i.), while the values in tables were presented as mean
\pm
standard deviation. We use the “Adam" optimizer
(
Kingma and Ba 2015
)
with the same Adam learning rate of 0.0003 and apply a uniform discount factor of
\gamma=0.99
to all tasks for consistency. Further experimental details can be found in Appendix
A
.

8.1
Comparison of Policy Optimization with and without VRER

In this section, we utilize four classical control benchmarks to demonstrate that the introduced VRER can effectively boost the performance of SOTA policy optimization methods, specifically
TRPO
(
Schulman et al. 2015
)
, PPO
(
Schulman et al. 2017
)
, and A2C
(Advantage Actor-Critic)
which represents a synchronous version of A3C (Asynchronous Advantage Actor-Critic)
(
Mnih et al. 2016
)
. We choose A2C instead of A3C as it has been found to have the same or better performance than the asynchronous version
(
Schulman et al. 2017
)
.

To represent the policy of interest,
both A2C and PPO used a fully-connected multilayer perceptron (MLP) with two shared hidden layers of 64 units, and tanh activation functions, while TRPO has separate actor and critic neural network models and both of them have two layers with 32 neurons. For the problems with discrete actions, we use the softmax activation function on top of the actor network, which calculates the probabilities of candidate actions. For the problem with continuous actions, we use the Gaussian policy for the actor model with fixed standard
deviations by following
Metelli et al. 2020
. The historical sample selection threshold is set to be
c=1.05
for all experiments in this section.

Figure 1:
Convergence results for the various PG algorithms with and without using VRER.

Figure
1
presents the mean performance curves along with 95% confidence intervals for all tested algorithms across four tasks. The results illuminate the substantial impact of using VRER on enhancing policy optimization algorithms. The findings unequivocally demonstrate the positive influence of VRER on the performance of SOTA policy optimization algorithms. In the case of A2C, the incorporation of VRER leads to a remarkable enhancement in both convergence speed and stability, except for the Lunar Lander task, where A2C faced challenges in convergence even with VRER assistance over 2 million steps. For the experiments involving the PPO algorithm, PPO-VRER shows not only enhanced stability but also accelerated convergence when compared to its non-VRER counterparts. Across all three scenarios, PPO-VRER exhibits a noteworthy improvement in performance. While the performance gains for TRPO may not be as significant as those seen with A2C and PPO, it is noteworthy that TRPO-VRER consistently outperforms its non-VRER counterparts across all four tasks. TRPO displays a superior average reward after 2 million steps, along with a more stable convergence trajectory.

Performance of the algorithms in terms of average reward (mean
\pm

different from the baseline algorithms in each task.

Table
1
presents the performance
of all these algorithms over last 10,000 timesteps, following
Schulman et al. 2017
(who considered the average total reward of the last 100 episodes). The efficacy of the VRER is consistently observed across different control tasks. Specifically, in the CartPole tasks, the performance gains from using VRER achieve 43% for PPO, 1% for TRPO, and 108.4% for A2C. In the Hopper task, VRER improves average rewards of PPO by 24.5%, TRPO by 23.3%, and A2C by 68.8% respectively. In the Inverted Pendulum tasks, VRER improves the average reward of PPO by 1.9%, TRPO by 5.1%, and A2C by 364.1% respectively. In the Lunar Lander tasks, VRER improves the average reward of PPO by 13.9%, TRPO by 32.3%, and A2C by 16.0% respectively.

To assess the performance of our PG-VRER approach relative to the established experience replay method within the actor-critic framework, we have taken the additional step of implementing the ACER algorithm. ACER, an abbreviation for “actor-critic with experience replay"
(
Wang et al. 2017
)
, represents an off-policy actor-critic model specifically designed to enhance sample efficiency and reduce data correlation. It is worth noting that ACER can be considered as A2C’s off-policy counterpart. We opted to implement only the version for discrete action spaces due to the challenges of implementing it for continuous action spaces, which was also mentioned in Stable-Baselines3
(
Raffin et al. 2021
)
, a PyTorch-based RL library. The convergence result for ACER can be found in Appendix
B
. These results demonstrate that our proposed PG-VRER outperforms state-of-the-art experience replay-based policy optimization methods.

In summary, the results in both Figure
1
and Table
1
underscore the significant benefits of employing VRER in policy optimization algorithms. VRER not only accelerates convergence but also enhances the overall stability and performance of these SOTA algorithms, offering a valuable tool for improved training efficiency and task-specific adaptability.

In this section, we study the impact of the value of selection constant
c
on the reusing pattern of historical samples.
Table

provides a comprehensive overview of the performance of VRER-based policy optimization algorithms in the context of the CartPole task under varying selection constants (
c
). We observe two key aspects: the reuse ratios (
|\mathcal{U}_{k}|/B
) and the average rewards obtained over one million steps.

The reuse ratios quantify the extent to which historical samples are selected and reused in the VRER-based policy optimization algorithms. Notably, as
c
increases from 1.00 to 1.60, the reuse ratios exhibit an upward trend for all three algorithms. When
c=1.001
, it represents an extreme scenario where the KL divergence between the behavior and target policies becomes close to zero, as indicated by the selection rule (
33
). In such cases, very few historical samples are eligible for reuse in VRER-based policy optimization algorithms. This extreme scenario significantly limits the algorithms’ capacity to utilize past experiences, resulting in a substantial reduction in their ability to benefit from historical data.

Another noteworthy observation from Table
2
is the relationship between the reuse ratio and the average reward. Typically we want the reuse ratio neither too high nor too low.
When the selection constant
c
is too large, the excessively high reuse ratio may lead to decreased performance as seen in PPO-VRER case. When
c
is too small, the low reuse ratio can also result in suboptimal average rewards, as shown in A2C-VRER.
These findings highlight the importance of selecting an appropriate reuse ratio, as deviations from the optimal range can impact the performance of VRER-based policy optimization algorithms. Achieving a balance between sample reuse and performance improvement is a crucial consideration in optimizing these algorithms.

8.3
Sensitivity Analysis of Buffer Size and Selection Constant

In this section, we study the effects of selection constant
c
and buffer size
B
on the performance of VRER. Figure
2
records the average rewards of (PPO,TRPO,A2C)-VRER with different buffer sizes.
Overall, the performance of VRER is robust to the selection of buffer size
B
. For PPO (-VRER), the convergence rate slows down noticeably as the buffer size is increased to 1,000 iterations (illustrated in pink) and 1,500 iterations (depicted in grey). These buffer sizes correspond to 1,152,000 and 2,304,000 transition steps, respectively, leading to the storage of all collected samples by VRER. In the case of TRPO, VRER’s performance remains consistent regardless of the buffer size. In contrast, for A2C-VRER, larger buffers appear to enhance the convergence rate. In specific,
A2C-VRER with larger buffer sizes, such as
B=1000,1500
, corresponding to 384,000 and 576,000 transition steps respectively, significantly outperforms those with smaller buffer sizes. In these experiments, the number of steps equals to
(B\times n\times\text{num-envs})
, where “num-envs" is the number of parallel environments; see Table
4
in Appendix
A
.

Figure 2:
Sensitivity analysis of buffer sizes. The average rewards achieved by PPO-VRER, TRPO-VRER, and A2C-VRER in the CartPole task with different buffer sizes.

Figure 3:
Sensitivity of the Selection Constant. The average rewards achieved by PPO-VRER, TRPO-VRER, and A2C-VRER in the CartPole task with different
c
.

To gain a deeper understanding of how the selection constant, denoted as
c
, influences VRER’s performance, we conducted supplementary experiments. We utilized various PG-VRER algorithms while employing the CartPole environment as a demonstration case.
Figure
3
displays the convergence results (mean and 95% c.i,) of (PPO, TRPO, A2C)-VRER over one million steps, using various values of the selection constant
c
. Notably, we observe differences in convergence patterns across different values of
c
. Specifically, PPO-VRER achieves its fastest convergence at
c=1.02
, while A2C-VRER exhibits the fastest convergence when
c=1.6
. In contrast, TRPO-VRER demonstrates relatively consistent convergence speed across different values of
c
. These results align with the findings presented in Table
2
, where we observe that the choice of the selection constant may be linked to the percentage of experience replayed, as indicated by the reuse ratio
|\mathcal{U}_{k}|/B
.

8.4
Gradient Variance Reduction

In this section, we present the empirical results to assess the performance of the proposed VRER in terms of reducing the policy gradient estimation variance.
We record the relative variance differences of all three algorithms in CartPole tasks without and with using VRER in Table
3
. In specific, Table

presents the average relative variance difference in policy gradient estimators when comparing policy optimization algorithms without and with VRER, i.e.,

, in the CartPole task under different selection constants. It shows that the relative variance of
\widehat{\nabla}J^{CLR}_{k}
is consistently lower than that of
\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}
across different selection constants over the training process except the extreme scenario
c=1.001
with limited reuse.

Overall, these results indicate that VRER has a notable influence on reducing the variance in policy gradient estimation. By selectively reusing historical samples through VRER, all three SOTA policy optimization algorithms show a significant reduction in the gradient variance. This reduction in gradient variance is a promising aspect of VRER, as it can contribute to more stable and efficient training of RL algorithms.

9
Conclusion

To improve the sample efficiency of policy optimization algorithms, we introduce a novel experience replay approach known as Variance Reduction Experience Replay (VRER) by integrating the classical experience replay method with a sample selection rule designed to reduce gradient variance.
By selectively reusing the most relevant historical samples and automatically allocating more weights to those samples that are more likely generated from the target distribution, VRER guarantees to reduce the policy gradient estimation variance. This integration represents a significant advancement in improving the efficiency of policy optimization algorithms according to both theoretical and empirical studies.

Despite their popularity, experience replay methods in RL lack theoretical investigations into their finite-time convergence and limiting properties, highlighting a gap in the literature. Our proposed theoretical framework addresses this gap by considering sample dependence due to Markovian noise and behavior policy interdependence. It elucidates the effectiveness of experience replay methods and explains (1) why reusing too old samples could reduce the policy optimization performance; and (2) how the performance is affected by the buffer size through a bias-variance trade-off in policy gradient estimation. By applying this new theory, we further study the finite-time convergence of the PG-VRER approach, providing a theoretical grounding for practical applications.

The proposed VRER is general and it can be seamlessly integrated with SOTA policy gradient approaches to improve their performance. In practice, it is simple to apply VRER as it does not require any structural change of the original algorithms (e.g., PPO, TRPO, and A2C); only by incorporating the selection rule before the training phase.
Extensive empirical studies have shown that our approach can substantially increase the convergence rate of SOTA policy gradient methods and enhance the performance of policy optimization.

Appendix A
Experiment Details

In this appendix, we report the hyperparameters,
and additional experimental results. In all comparison experiments, each pair of baseline and VRER-based algorithm were run under the same settings. To reproduce the results and check out the implementation details, please visit our repository:
https://github.com/zhenghuazx/vrer_policy_gradient
.

A.1
Hyperparameters

•

Policy architecture
: For discrete action, we adopted a softmax policy:
for a
m
-dimensional action,

\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(a_{i}|\boldsymbol{s})=\frac{\exp\left(\phi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{s},a_{i})\right)}{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\exp\left(\phi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{s},a_{i}^{\prime})\right)}.
The mean function
\phi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is a 2–layers MLP with ReLU activation for hidden–layers, and linear for output layer).

For continuous actions, we adopted a Gaussian policy: the mean function
is a 2–layer MLP with tanh activation for hidden layers and linear for output layer. The variance is state–independent and parametrized identity matrix).

•

Number of macro-replications
: 8.

•

Policy initialization
: orthogonal initialization for all policies in our experiments.

The VRER-related hyperparameters include selection constant
c
, the buffer size
B
, and the number of sampled observations per iteration
n_{0}
. The environmental condition includes “num-envs" which represents the number of parallel environments (each iteration, each environment collect
n
steps of data) and the learning rate for policy optimization.

The A2C(-VRER) hyperparameters are presented in Table
4
, where “entropy coef" and“value loss coef" represents the entropy coefficient and value loss coefficient for value loss calculation respectively. The PPO(-VRER) hyperparameters are presented in Table
5
. In specific,“mini batches" represents the number of mini-batch; “batch size" represents the number of transitions per iteration; “entropy coef" represents the entropy coefficient used for entropy loss calculation; and “Lambda" represents the lambda for the general advantage estimation. The “PPO iterations" hyperparameter (
K_{off}
) represents the maximum number of actor-critic optimization steps per train step. The TRPO(-VRER) hyperparameters are presented in Table
6
. The hyperparameter “cg iterations" represents the gradient conjugation maximum number of iterations per train step; “entropy coef" represents the entropy coefficient used for entropy loss calculation; the “actor/critic iterations" hyperparameter (
K_{off}
) represents the maximum number of actor/critic optimizations steps per train step; and “clip norm" represents the surrogate clipping coefficient.

Appendix B
Additional Experimental Results

To validate our approach, we further perform experiments on two discrete control tasks to compare the convergence between A2C-VRER and ACER. Both approaches are implemented based on the A2C algorithm. For a fair comparison, we keep the environmental conditions fixed for all three algorithms (0.02 clip norm, 0.01 entropy coef, 16 n-steps, 0.0003 learning rate, 24 num-envs for CartPole and 48 num-envs for Lunar Lander) and then tune the hyperparameter for ACER including the maximum buffer size, replay ratio, and the importance weight truncation parameter. For detailed specifics of ACER, please refer to our open-sourced implementation and
Wang et al. 2017
.

Appendix C
Proof of Preliminary Lemmas

C.1
Key Proof Technique

The key proof techniques in Theorems
3
and
5
rely on employing the uniform ergodicity assumption (
A.2
). This technique was
first introduced by
Bhandari et al. 2018
to address the Markovian noise in policy evaluation.

Zou et al. 2019
extended its usage to the Q-learning setting and
Wu et al. 2020
further considered the situation with the policy parameter changing. In this work, we take two steps further: (1) reusing historical samples collected under old behavior policies, thereby causing a complex dependence structure; and (2) using a simple but biased LR-based gradient estimate (i.e., ratio of target and behavior policies), which introduces additional theoretical difficulty.

Suppose that a historical sample
\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)}=\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}\right)
is selected and replayed at iteration
k
with
i<k
. Due to Markovian noise and policy updates, all samples are correlated and their dependencies can be analyzed by using the Auxiliary Markov Chain (AMC) and Stationary Markov Chain (SMC) constructed below.
To elucidate the effects of Markovian noise, policy update, and historical sample reuse, AMC and SMC will be utilized along with uniform ergodicity and triangle inequality to bound the dependency effect induced by Markovian noise and experience reply in the proof of Theorems
3
and
5
.

Original Markov Chain
: For reference, we first present the original Markov chain with policy update, i.e.,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}^{(i-t,1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{a}^{(i-t,1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\boldsymbol{s}^{(i-t,j)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{a}^{(i-t,j)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{s}^{(i-t,n)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i-t,n)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}}

\displaystyle{\cdots}

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,n)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i-t,n)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}^{(k,1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{a}^{(k,1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\boldsymbol{s}^{(k,j+1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{a}^{(k,j+1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{s}^{(k,n)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(k,n)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots

(35)

where
\mathbb{P}
represents the state transition model on
\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}\in\cdot|\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})
and
t
is an integer with
0\leq t<i
.

Auxiliary Markov Chain (AMC)
: The analysis of Markovian noise relies on the
auxiliary Markov chain introduced by
Zou et al. 2019
which constructs a state-action sequence by following a fixed policy
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}
with
0\leq t<i
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}^{(i-t,1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i-t,1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i-t,j)}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i-t,j)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i-t,n)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}^{i-t,n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i-t+1,1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i-t+1,1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i-t+1,n)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}^{i-t,n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\cdots}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i,1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i,1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i,j)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i,j)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ldots

(36)

Since a fixed policy
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}
is used, the highlighted trajectory part associates with the difference comparing with original Markov chain with policy updates.

Stationary Markov Chain (SMC)
: The analysis of the interdependence of behavior policies relies on bounding the distance between their policy parameters. To achieve this, we construct a hypothetical sample path (highlighted trajectory part below) wherein each sample in the path is independently drawn from the fixed stationary distribution
(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\sim d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})
. It is worth noting that the chain we created is no longer a Markov chain but a sequence of independent samples.

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i-t,1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i-t,1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\check{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i-t,j)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\check{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i-t,j)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\check{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i-t,n)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\check{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i-t,n)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\check{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i-t+1,1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t+1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\check{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i-t+1,1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\check{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i-t+1,n)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\check{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i-t+1,n)}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\cdots}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\left(\check{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i,1)},\check{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i,1)}\right),\cdots,\left(\check{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i,j)},\check{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i,j)}\right)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots

(37)

We can concatenate the nested chains (
35
) and (
36
) by reindexing the trajectory
t\leftarrow nt
and
\tau\leftarrow n(i-1)+j
such that

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau-t},\boldsymbol{a}_{\tau-t})\leftarrow\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{(i-t,j)},\boldsymbol{a}^{(i-t,j)}\right)\text{ and }(\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau},\boldsymbol{a}_{\tau})\leftarrow\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}\right).

As a result, the subsequence from
\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{(i-t,j)},\boldsymbol{a}^{(i-t,j)}\right)
to
\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}\right)
in Markov chains (
36
) and (
35
) can be concatenated into a single chain shown below.

(Concatenated) Auxilary Markov Chain:

\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\tau-t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\tau-t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\tau-t+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\tau-t+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\tau-t+2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\tau-t+2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\tau}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\tau}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\tau+1}.

(38)

(Concatenated) Original Markov Chain:

\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau-t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau-t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{a}_{\tau-t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau-t+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau-t+1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{a}_{\tau-t+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau-t+2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau-t+2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{a}_{\tau-t+2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{a}_{\tau}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau+1}.

(39)

Therefore, the existing results on the concatenated AMC (single chain MDP) can be directly transferred to our setting. We list those results as supportive lemmas in Section
C.3

C.2
Notations for Main Theorems

We use
\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)}=(\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i,j)},\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i,j)})
to denote a state-action pair sample generated from the Auxiliary Markov Chain (AMC) that constructs a state-action sequence by following a fixed policy
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}
with
0\leq t<i
.
Let
\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)}=(\check{\boldsymbol{s}}^{(i,j)},\check{\boldsymbol{a}}^{(i,j)})
denote a sample generated from the stationary distribution
d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})
.
We define some notations here for clarification.

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}_{t}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{s}_{t},\boldsymbol{a}_{t})

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)}g\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)}g\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}J^{CLR}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\min\left(\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)},U_{f}\right)g\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}J^{CLR}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)

\displaystyle=\min\left(\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)},U_{f}\right)g\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}J\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=g\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}J\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=g\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}J\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=g\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}),\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\rangle

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}),\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t})\right\rangle

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}),\widehat{\nabla}J\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t})\right\rangle

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}),\widehat{\nabla}J\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t})\right\rangle

C.3
Preliminary Supportive Lemmas

The proof of the following lemma is similar to the proof of Lemma B.2 in
Wu et al. 2020
with the difference that
Wu et al. 2020
considers the case with finite action space.

Lemma 3

(Lipschitz Continuity of Stationary Distribution)
.

Given time indexes
t
and
\tau
such that
\tau\geq t\geq 0
, we consider the concatenated original Markov chain defined in (
39
) and auxiliary Markov chain (
38
). Conditioning on
\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau-t+1}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau-t}
, we have

\displaystyle\|

\displaystyle p(\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau+1}|\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau-t+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau-t})-p(\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\tau+1}|\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau-t+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau-t})\|_{TV}

\displaystyle\leq\|p(\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau},\boldsymbol{a}_{\tau}|\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau-t+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau-t})-p(\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\tau},\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\tau}|\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau-t+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau-t})\|_{TV}

(40)

\displaystyle\|

\displaystyle p(\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau},\boldsymbol{a}_{\tau}|\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau-t+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau-t})-p(\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\tau},\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\tau}|\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau-t+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau-t})\|_{TV}

\displaystyle\leq\|p(\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau}|\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau-t+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau-t})-p(\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\tau}|\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau-t+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau-t})\|_{TV}+U_{\pi}{\mathbb{E}}[\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\tau-t}\|]

(41)

Proof.

The proof for (
40
) and (
41
) is identical to that of Lemma B.2 in
Wu et al. 2020
. The only difference is that the proof in
Wu et al. 2020
handles a discrete action space and uses
\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
, but our lemma considers both discrete and continuous action space, employing the integral notation
\int_{\mathcal{A}}
to traverse this space.

∎

Lemma
4
and
5
shows the Lipschitz continuity of the stationary distribution and action value function. Similar supportive lemmas appear in many policy search algorithms
(
Xu et al. 2020
;
Zou et al. 2019
;
Wu et al. 2020
)
. Readers are referred to
Xu et al. 2020
for detailed proofs of these two lemmas.

Lemma 4

(
Xu et al. 2020
, Lemma 3)
.

Consider the stationary distribution of the state-action pair
d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(s,a)
.
For any
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
it holds that

\|d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}}(\cdot,\cdot)-d^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\cdot,\cdot)\|_{TV}\leq C_{d}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\|

where
C_{d}=U_{\pi}(1+\lceil\log_{\kappa}\kappa_{0}^{-1}\rceil+\frac{1}{1-\kappa})
.

Lemma 5

(
Xu et al. 2020
, Lemma 4)
.

Suppose Assumptions
A.1
and
A.2
hold. For any
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any state-action pair
(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, we have

|Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})-Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})|\leq L_{Q}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\|

where
L_{Q}=\frac{2U_{r}C_{d}}{1-\gamma}

Lemma 6

(Lipchitz Continuity of Advantage Function)
.

Suppose Assumptions
A.1
and
A.2
hold. For any
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any state-action pair
(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, we have

|A^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})-A^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})|\leq L_{A}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\|

where
L_{A}=2L_{Q}+U_{J}U_{\pi}
,
L_{Q}=\frac{2U_{r}C_{d}}{1-\gamma}
, and
U_{J}=U_{r}/(1-\gamma)
.

Proof.

To begin with, we show the Lipchitz continuity of state value function,

\displaystyle|V^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}}(\boldsymbol{s})-V^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s})|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\mathcal{A}}Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{a}-\int_{\mathcal{A}}Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{a}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{A}}|Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})-Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})|\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{a}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathcal{A}}\left|Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\right|\left|\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})-\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})\right|\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{a}.

By applying Lemma
5
, it holds that

\int_{\mathcal{A}}|Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})-Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})|\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{a}\leq L_{Q}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\|\int_{\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{a}=L_{Q}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\|.

By applying Eq. (
10
) and Assumption
A.1
, it holds that

\int_{\mathcal{A}}\left|Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\right|\left|\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})-\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})\right|\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{a}\leq U_{J}U_{\pi}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\|.

Therefore, we have
|V^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}}(\boldsymbol{s})-V^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s})|\leq(L_{Q}+U_{J}U_{\pi})\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\|
. Now we prove the Lipchitz continuity of advantage function. By triangle inequality, it holds that

\displaystyle|A^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})-A^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})|

\displaystyle=|Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})-V^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}}(\boldsymbol{s})-Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})+V^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s})|

\displaystyle\leq|Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})-Q^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})|+|V^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}}(\boldsymbol{s})-V^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s})|

\displaystyle\leq(2L_{Q}+U_{J}U_{\pi})\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\|,

(42)

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 7

(Lipchitz Continuity of Sample Gradient)
.

Suppose Assumptions
A.1
and
A.2
hold. For any
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any state-action pair
(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, we have

\|g(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})-g(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\|\leq L_{g}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\|

where
L_{g}=U_{\Theta}L_{A}+2U_{J}L_{\Theta}
.

Proof.

By applying Eq. (
4
) and Minkowski’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|g(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})-g(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\|

\displaystyle=\|A^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\nabla\log\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})-A^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\nabla\log\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})\|

\displaystyle=\|A^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\nabla\log\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})-A^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\nabla\log\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle\quad+A^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\nabla\log\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})-A^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})\nabla\log\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})\|

\displaystyle\leq|A^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})-A^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})|\|\nabla\log\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})\|

\displaystyle\quad+|A^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})|\|\nabla\log\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})-\nabla\log\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{s})\|.

Notice that
|A^{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a})|\leq 2U_{J}
holds because both state- and action-value functions are bounded by
U_{J}
, as shown in Eq. (
10
) and (
11
). By applying Lemma
6
and Assumption
A.1
, we have

\|g(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})-g(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{a}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\|\leq U_{\Theta}L_{A}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\|+2U_{J}L_{\Theta}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\|=L_{g}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\|

where
L_{g}=U_{\Theta}L_{A}+2U_{J}L_{\Theta}
.
∎

Appendix D
Proofs of Main Theorems

D.1
Proof of Theorem
3

Theorem
3
(Gradient Biasedness)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
A.1
and
A.2
hold. For both LR and CLR policy gradient estimators in Eq. (
15
) and (
16
), we consider the policy update rule (
2
) with the learning rate
\eta_{k}
. For any integer
t
such that
0\leq t<i
, the bias of policy gradient estimators (R
\in
{
LR,CLR
}
) can be bounded by

\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})]\right\|\leq\frac{Z_{1}^{R}(t+1)}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{i}_{\ell=i-t}\eta_{\ell}+2M\varphi(nt)+\frac{Z_{2}^{R}}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{k}_{\ell=i-t}\eta_{\ell},

where
Z_{1}^{LR}=2nM^{2}U_{\pi}
,
Z_{1}^{CLR}=4nM^{2}U_{\pi}U_{L}U_{f}
,
Z_{2}^{LR}=M(2MC_{d}+ML_{g}+U_{L}L_{g}+MU_{\pi})
and
Z_{2}^{CLR}=M(2MC_{d}+ML_{g}+U_{f}L_{g}+MU_{\pi})
. Here,
C_{d}
and
L_{g}
are defined in Lemma
4
and Lemma
7
respectively in Appendix
C.3
.

Proof.

(i)
We start with the proof for the LR policy gradient estimator. Notice that

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR}_{k}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})]\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|n}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{n}_{j=1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)}g\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|n}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{n}_{j=1}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)}g\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]\right\|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|n}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{n}_{j=1}\left\|\Delta_{\textbf{(i)}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\|,

(43)

where
\Delta_{\textbf{(i)}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})={\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)}g\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]
can be decomposed by

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta_{\textbf{(i)}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR}\left({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)-\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR}\left({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR}\left({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)-\widehat{\nabla}J\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)-\widehat{\nabla}J\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)-\widehat{\nabla}J\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)-\nabla J\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)\right]\right\|.

(44)

By applying Lemma
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
and
13
to each term in (
44
), we have

\left\|\Delta_{\textbf{(i)}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\|\leq 2M\varphi(nt)+2nM^{2}U_{\pi}t\sum^{i}_{\ell=i-t}\eta_{\ell}+M(2MC_{d}+L_{g}M+U_{L}L_{g}+MU_{\pi})\sum^{k}_{\ell=i-t}\eta_{\ell}.

(45)

The conclusion follows by plugging the upper bound (
45
) to (
43
).

(ii)
The proof for the CLR policy gradient estimator is similar to that of LR one. First,

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{CLR}_{k}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})]\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|n}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{n}_{j=1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\min\left(\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)},U_{f}\right)g\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|n}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{n}_{j=1}\left\|\Delta_{\textbf{(ii)}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\|

(46)

where
\Delta_{\textbf{(ii)}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})={\mathbb{E}}\left[\min\left(\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{s}^{(i,j)}\right)},U_{f}\right)g\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]
. Then we decompose
\Delta_{\textbf{(ii)}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})
by

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta_{\textbf{(ii)}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{CLR}\left({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)-\widehat{\nabla}J^{CLR}\left({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{CLR}\left({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)-\widehat{\nabla}J\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)-\widehat{\nabla}J\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)-\widehat{\nabla}J\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)-\nabla J\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)\right]\right\|

By applying Lemma
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
and
13
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4nM^{2}U_{\pi}U_{L}U_{f}(t+1)\sum^{i}_{\ell=i-t}\eta_{\ell}+2M\varphi(nt)+M(2MC_{d}+L_{g}M+U_{f}L_{g}+MU_{\pi})\sum^{k}_{\ell=i-t}\eta_{\ell}.

(47)

The conclusion follows by obtained by plugging the upper bound (
D.1
) to (
46
).
∎

D.2
Proof of Proposition
1

Proposition
1
(Gradient Variance)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
A.1
and
A.2
hold. At the
k
-th iteration with the target policy
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}
, given the reuse set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
that includes behavior policies, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
, the total variance of LR/CLR policy gradient estimators (
R\in
{
LR,CLR
}
) in Eq. (
15
) and (
16
) can be decomposed to

{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]\right)=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{R}_{i,k}\right)^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{R}_{i^{\prime},k}\right)

where
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{R}_{i,k}=\left(\sqrt{{\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R,(1)}_{i,k}\right]},\sqrt{{\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R,(2)}_{i,k}\right]},\ldots,\sqrt{{\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R,(d)}_{i,k}\right]}\right)^{\top}
and

\mathbf{P}_{i,i^{\prime},k}={\rm Diag}\left({\rm Corr}^{(1)}_{i,i^{\prime},k},\ldots,{\rm Corr}^{(d)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right)
with correlation coefficients of each gradient estimate pair
{\rm Corr}_{i,i^{\prime},k}^{(\ell)}={\rm Corr}\left(\widehat{\nabla}J^{R,(\ell)}_{i,k},\widehat{\nabla}J^{R,(\ell)}_{i^{\prime},k}\right)

for each
\ell
-th dimension of policy parameters with
\ell=1,2,\ldots,d
.
By using the greatest element of
\mathbf{P}
, the total variance (
26
) is bounded by

{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]\right)\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\max_{\ell=1,2,\ldots,d}\left({\rm Corr}^{(\ell)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{R}_{i,k}\right)^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{R}_{i^{\prime},k}\right).

Proof.

Due to the Markovian noise and policy update, the observations
\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)}
for any
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}
and
j=1,2,\ldots,n
are dependent. Thus, we have

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{i,k}\right]\right)

(48)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Cov}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{i,k},\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{i^{\prime},k}\right]\right).

Due to the sample dependence, policy gradient estimates are dependent. Let
{\rm Corr}_{i,i^{\prime},k}^{(\ell)}={\rm Corr}\left(\widehat{\nabla}J^{R,(\ell)}_{i,k},\widehat{\nabla}J^{R,(\ell)}_{i^{\prime},k}\right)
denote the correlation coefficient of the
\ell
-th element between two individual LR/CLR policy gradient estimates. Then the total variance (
48
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{d}_{\ell=1}{\rm Corr}^{(\ell)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\sqrt{{\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R,(\ell)}_{i,k}\right]}\sqrt{{\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R,(\ell)}_{i^{\prime},k}\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{R}_{i,k}\right)^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{R}_{i^{\prime},k}\right)

(49)

where
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{R}_{i,k}=\left(\sqrt{{\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R,(1)}_{i,k}\right]},\sqrt{{\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R,(2)}_{i,k}\right]},\ldots,\sqrt{{\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R,(d)}_{i,k}\right]}\right)^{\top}
and

\mathbf{P}_{i,i^{\prime},k}={\rm Diag}\left({\rm Corr}^{(1)}_{i,i^{\prime},k},\ldots,{\rm Corr}^{(d)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}{\rm Corr}^{(1)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}&0&\ldots&0\\
0&{\rm Corr}^{(2)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&{\rm Corr}^{(d)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\end{pmatrix}
.
∎

D.3
Proof of Theorems
4

Theorem
4
.

At the
k
-th iteration with the target distribution
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}
, the reuse set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
is created to include the behavioral distributions, i.e.,
\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}
with
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
,
whose total variance of individual LR/CLR policy gradient estimators in (
14
) and
(
17
)
is no greater than
c
times the total variance of the classical PG estimator for some constant
c>1
. Mathematically, for
R\in
{
LR,CLR
}
,

\text{{Selection Rule 1:}}~~~{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{i,k}\right]\right)\leq c{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\right]\right).

Then, based on such reuse set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
, the total variances of the average LR/CLR policy gradient estimators (
15
) and (
16
) are no greater than the total variance of the PG estimator scaled by the averaged max correlation between individual LR/CLR policy gradient estimates,

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]\right)\leq\frac{c}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\right]\right)\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\max_{\ell=1,2,\ldots,d}\left({\rm Corr}^{(\ell)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right).

Moreover, it holds that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\frac{c}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\max_{\ell=1,2,\ldots,d}\left({\rm Corr}^{(\ell)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right){\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+2\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]\right\|^{2}+2\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})]\right\|^{2}.

Proof.

To begin with, following Proposition
1
, we have

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{R}_{i,k}\right)^{\top}\mathbf{P}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{R}_{i^{\prime},k}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\max_{\ell=1,2,\ldots,d}\left({\rm Corr}^{(\ell)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{R}_{i,k}\right)^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{R}_{i^{\prime},k}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\max_{\ell=1,2,\ldots,d}\left({\rm Corr}^{(\ell)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right){\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\right]\right)

(50)

where (
50
) holds because of selection rule (
28
).
Thus, (
29
) is proved.
Then, we have

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]\right)

(51)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}\left({\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right)\left(\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right)^{\top}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right\|^{2}\right]-\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right\|^{2}\right]-2\|{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})]\|^{2}-2\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\left[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]\right\|^{2}.

Similarly, we have

{\rm Tr}\left({\rm Var}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\right]\right)={\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\right\|^{2}\right]-\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\right]\right\|^{2}\leq{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\right\|^{2}\right].

(52)

Then by applying inequality (
50
)–(
52
) and rearranging the both sides, we can show

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\max_{\ell=1,2,\ldots,d}\left({\rm Corr}^{(\ell)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right){\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+2\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]\right\|^{2}+2\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\left[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]\right\|^{2}

which completes the proof.
∎

D.4
Proof of Theorem
5

Lemma 8
.

Suppose Assumptions
A.1
and
A.2
hold. Let
\eta_{k}=\eta_{1}k^{-r}
denote the learning rate used in the
k
-th iteration with two constants
\eta_{1}\in(0,\frac{1}{4L}]
and
r\in(0,1)
, where
L
is defined in Lemma
1
. By running Algorithm
1
, for both LR and CLR policy gradient estimators, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\eta_{k}}\left({\mathbb{E}}\left[J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1})\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\left[J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]\right)+2(I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3})

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\left(C_{1}(k-i+t)\eta_{i-t}+C_{2}(t+1)t\eta_{i-t}+2M^{2}\varphi(nt)\right)

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{cLM^{2}\eta_{k}}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\max_{\ell=1,2,\ldots,d}\left({\rm Corr}^{(\ell)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right)

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=2L\eta_{k}\|{\rm Bias}_{k}\|^{2}

where
C_{1}=\max\{C^{\Gamma}_{1},C^{\Gamma}_{2}\}
,
C_{2}={4nM^{2}U_{\pi}U_{L}U_{f}}/(1-r)
, and
{\rm Bias}_{k}={\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\left[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]
with
R\in\{LR,CLR\}
. Here
C_{1}^{\Gamma}
and
C_{2}^{\Gamma}
are defined in Lemma
14
and
Z^{LR}_{2}
is defined in Theorem
3
.
c
is the selection constant defined in Theorem
4
.

Proof.

Lemma
1
implies the L-Lipschitz property of policy gradient, which by the definition of smoothness (
Nesterov 2003
, Lemma 1.2.3) is also equivalent to

J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})-J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1})\leq\langle\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}),\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1}\rangle+L\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\|^{2}.

Let
\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}
represents either LR policy gradient estimator
\widehat{\nabla}J^{LR}_{k}
or CLR policy gradient estimator
\widehat{\nabla}J^{CLR}_{k}
, (i.e.
R\in\{LR,CLR\}
). By applying the policy parameter update implemented in the proposed algorithm
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}+\eta_{k}\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}
, we have

\displaystyle J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})-J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1})\leq-\left\langle\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}),\eta_{k}\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\rangle+L\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\eta_{k}\left\langle\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}),\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})-\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\rangle-\eta_{k}\|\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\|^{2}+L\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\|^{2}.

(53)

Then by taking the expectation of both sides of (
53
), we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\left[J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\eta_{k}\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}),\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\rangle\right]\right|-{\eta_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\|^{2}\right]+L{\mathbb{E}}[\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\eta_{k}\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}),\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\rangle\right]\right|-{\eta_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\|^{2}\right]+L\eta_{k}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right\|^{2}\right].

(54)

By applying Theorem
4
and rearranging both sides, we have

\displaystyle(1-2L\eta_{k}){\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\eta_{k}}\left({\mathbb{E}}\left[J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1})\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\left[J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}),\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\rangle\right]\right|}_{I_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\frac{cL\eta_{k}}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\max_{\ell=1,2,\ldots,d}\left({\rm Corr}^{(\ell)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right){\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\right\|^{2}\right]}_{I_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{2L\eta_{k}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\|^{2}}_{I_{3}}.

(55)

For term
I_{1}
, we have

I_{1}=\left|\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|n}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{n}_{j=1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}),\frac{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}\right)}{\pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{a}^{(i,j)}\right)}g\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\rangle\right]\right|.

By applying Jensen’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{j=1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}),\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\rangle\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{j=1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Gamma\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\right)-\Gamma\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|n}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{n}_{j=1}\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Gamma\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)-\Gamma\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|n}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{n}_{j=1}\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Gamma\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)-\Gamma\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|n}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{n}_{j=1}\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Gamma\left(\check{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i-t}\right)\right]\right|.

By applying Lemma
14
, Lemma
15
, Lemma
16
, and Lemma
17
, it follows that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{j=1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}),\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\rangle\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq C^{\Gamma}_{1}(k-i+t)^{1/2}\eta_{i-t}+C_{2}^{\Gamma}(k-i+t)\eta_{i-t}+4nM^{2}U_{\pi}U_{L}U_{f}(t+1)t\eta_{i-t}+2M^{2}\varphi(nt)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}(k-i+t)\eta_{i-t}+{4nM^{3}U_{\pi}U_{L}U_{f}}(t+1)t\eta_{i-t}+2M^{2}\varphi(nt)

(56)

where
C_{1}=\max\{C^{\Gamma}_{1},C^{\Gamma}_{2}\}
. The first term of Step (
56
) is due to the fact that
(k-i+t)^{1/2}\leq k-i+t
. The second term of Step (
56
) follows from noticing that
\sum^{i}_{\ell=i-t}\eta_{\ell}\leq t\eta_{i-t}
. Let
C_{2}={4nM^{3}U_{\pi}U_{L}U_{f}}
. Then term
I_{1}
becomes

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{j=1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}),\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(i,j)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})-\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\rangle\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\left(C_{1}(k-i+t)\eta_{i-t}+C_{2}(t+1)t\eta_{i-t}+2M^{2}\varphi(nt)\right).

For term
I_{2}
, by noticing
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\widehat{\nabla}J^{PG}_{k}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{j=1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|g(\boldsymbol{s}^{(k,j)},\boldsymbol{a}^{(k,j)}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq M^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{cLM^{2}\eta_{k}}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\max_{\ell=1,2,\ldots,d}\left({\rm Corr}^{(\ell)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right).

(57)

For term
I_{3}
, by applying Theorem
3
, we have

I_{3}=2L\eta_{k}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\left[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]\right\|^{2}=2L\eta_{k}\|{\rm Bias}_{k}\|^{2}.

(58)

where the bias is a convergent sequence given by Theorem
3
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|{\rm Bias}_{k}\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Z_{1}^{R}(t+1)}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{i}_{\ell=i-t}\eta_{\ell}+2M\varphi(nt)+\frac{Z_{2}^{R}}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum^{k}_{\ell=i-t}\eta_{\ell}.

Consider
\eta_{k}
small enough that
1-2L\eta_{k}\geq\frac{1}{2}
or equivalently
\eta_{k}\leq\frac{1}{4L}
. As the learning rate is non-increasing, this condition can be simplified as the initial learning rate
\eta_{1}\leq\frac{1}{4L}
. Then it proceeds with

\left(1-2L\eta_{k}\right){\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\|^{2}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\|^{2}\right].

Thus, the bound (
55
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\eta_{k}}\left({\mathbb{E}}\left[J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1})\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\left[J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]\right)+I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}

(59)

which completes the proof.
∎

Theorem
5

Suppose Assumptions
A.1
and
A.2
hold. Let
\eta_{k}=\eta_{1}k^{-r}
denote the learning rate used in the
k
-th iteration with two constants
\eta_{1}\in(0,\frac{1}{4L}]
and
r\in(0,1)
. By running Algorithm
1
(with the replay buffer of size
B_{K}
), for both LR/CLR policy gradient estimators in Eq. (
15
) and (
16
)and
t\leq K-B_{K}
, we have the rate of convergence

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K}\sum^{K}_{k=1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8U_{J}/\eta_{1}}{K^{1-r}}+\frac{4cLM^{2}}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\eta_{k}}\bar{\rho}_{k}+{4M^{2}}\varphi(nt)+\frac{2^{r+1}C_{3}}{(1-r)K^{r}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2^{r+1}C_{2}\eta_{1}(t+1)t}{(1-r)K^{r}}+\frac{2^{r+1}C_{1}\eta_{1}}{1-r}\frac{B_{K}+t}{K^{r}}+M^{2}\frac{B_{K}+t}{K}

where
C_{1}=\max\{C^{\Gamma}_{1},C^{\Gamma}_{2}\}
,
C_{2}={4nM^{3}U_{\pi}U_{L}U_{f}}
,
C_{3}=\sup_{k\geq 1}\|{\rm Bias}_{k}\|
with
{\rm Bias}_{k}={\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{\nabla}J^{R}_{k}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\left[\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]
and
\bar{\rho}_{k}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\left|\max_{\ell=1,2,\ldots,d}\left({\rm Corr}^{(\ell)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right)\right|
.
Here
R\in\{LR,CLR\},

C_{1}^{\Gamma}
and
C_{2}^{\Gamma}
are defined in Lemma
14
,
and
c
is the selection constant defined in Theorem
4
.
Using
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
notation gives

\frac{1}{K}\sum^{K}_{k=1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\|^{2}\right]\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{K^{1-r}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\varphi(nt)\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{t^{2}}{K^{r}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{B_{K}+t}{K^{r}}\right)

where
n
is the number of steps in each iteration. The notation

\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
hides constants
c
,
L
,
\eta_{1}
,
M
,
U_{J}
,
n
,
U_{f}
,
U_{\pi}
,
U_{L}
,
\kappa_{0}
,
\kappa
and
L_{g}
.

Proof.

For the dynamic buffer with size
B_{k}>0
, it holds that
k-i\leq B_{k}
for any
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}
. Here the buffer size is an increasing function of
k
.
For the first term on the right-hand side of Lemma
8
, we have

\displaystyle\sum^{K}_{k=B_{K}+t}\frac{1}{\eta_{k}}\left({\mathbb{E}}\left[J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k+1})\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\left[J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\eta_{B_{K}+t}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{B_{K}+t})\right]+\frac{1}{\eta_{K}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{K+1})\right]+\sum_{k=B_{K}+t+1}^{K}\left(\frac{1}{\eta_{k-1}}-\frac{1}{\eta_{k}}\right){\mathbb{E}}\left[J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\eta_{B_{K}+t}}U_{J}+\frac{1}{\eta_{K}}U_{J}+\sum_{k=B_{K}+t+1}^{K}\left(\frac{1}{\eta_{k}}-\frac{1}{\eta_{k-1}}\right)U_{J}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\eta_{K}}U_{J}.

(60)

Then, by applying Lemma
8
and noticing
|J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})|\leq U_{J}
and summing over
k=B_{K}+t,B_{K}+t+1,\ldots,K
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=B_{K}+t}^{K}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\nabla J(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\|^{2}\right]\leq\frac{{2}}{\eta_{K}}U_{J}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\sum^{K}_{k=B_{K}+t}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\left(C_{1}(k-i+t)\eta_{i-t}+C_{2}(t+1)t\eta_{i-t}+2M^{2}\varphi(nt)\right)}_{I_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{cLM^{2}\sum_{k=B_{K}+t}^{K}\frac{\eta_{k}}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\max_{\ell=1,2,\ldots,d}\left({\rm Corr}^{(\ell)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right)}_{I_{2}}+\underbrace{2L\sum^{K}_{k=B_{K}+t}\eta_{k}\|{\rm Bias}_{k}\|}_{I_{3}}.

(61)

For term
I_{1}
: Noticing that (1)
k-i\leq B_{k}\leq B_{K}
for any
K\geq k\geq i
and (2)
\eta_{i-t}=\frac{\eta_{1}}{(i-t)^{r}}\leq\frac{\eta_{1}}{(k-B_{K}-t)^{r}}
. Thus, it holds that

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum^{K}_{k=B_{K}+t}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}_{k}|}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\in\mathcal{U}_{k}}\left(\frac{C_{1}\eta_{1}(B_{K}+t)}{(k-B_{K}-t)^{r}}+\frac{C_{2}\eta_{1}(t+1)t}{(k-B_{K}-t)^{r}}+2M^{2}\varphi(nt)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum^{K}_{k=B_{K}+t}\left(\frac{C_{1}\eta_{1}(B_{K}+t)}{(k-B_{K}-t)^{r}}+\frac{C_{2}\eta_{1}(t+1)t}{(k-B_{K}-t)^{r}}+2M^{2}\varphi(nt)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{1}\eta_{1}(B_{K}+t)}{1-r}(K-B_{K}-t)^{1-r}+\frac{C_{2}\eta_{1}(t+1)t}{1-r}(K-B_{K}-t)^{1-r}

\displaystyle\quad+{2M^{2}}\varphi(nt)(K-B_{K}-t)

where the last step holds due to the fact that

\sum_{k=B_{K}+t}^{K}\frac{1}{(k-B_{K}-t)^{r}}\leq\int^{K-B_{K}-t}_{0}\frac{\eta_{1}}{k^{r}}\mathrm{d}k=\frac{\eta_{1}}{1-r}(K-B_{K}-t)^{1-r}.

For term
I_{2}
: Notice that
\max_{\ell=1,2,\ldots,d}\left({\rm Corr}^{(\ell)}_{i,i^{\prime},k}\right)\leq 1
. Then we have
